21 Food industries, due to the nature of their production, are identified as important   22 consumers of high-quality water. Likewise, their wastewater production is             23 high, and this forces the food industry to consider water resource preservation ub [SOURCE: Page_2]

24 as a strategic and vital priority. Water cannot be considered as a common             25 fluid, but as a fundamental raw material to ensure the quality and safety of          26 products (Mathieu-André, 2000). At each level (production, cleaning, cooling,         27 etc.), water management consists in controlling and reducing water consumption dP [SOURCE: Page_2]

28 and reducing effluent. Whatever the potential ways to reduce water                    29 consumption, by acting on the production process or on the effluent treatment,        30 ready-to-use or plug-and-play solutions do not exist. Each process and each           31 product requires specific analysis to match different motivations and constraints     32 (cost control or reduction, environmental constraints). ea [SOURCE: Page_2]

33 The brewing, winemaking and distilling industries produce alcohol as a             34 beverage, industrial solvent or fuel. These three processes exhibit strong            35 similarities (fermentation and separation operations) and stand as important          36 dh [SOURCE: Page_2]

water consumers and wastewater producers. In the food industry, the brewing,          37 winemaking and distilling (spirit production) sectors hold a strategic economic       38 position with world production estimated at 159.8 109 L beer, 26.7 109 L              39 wine and 7.0 109 L spirits expressed in LPA/y (LPA = litre of pure alcohol)           40 o [SOURCE: Page_2]

in 2004 (see Table 35.1).                                                             41 Beer is the fifth most widely consumed beverage in the world behind tea,           42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_2]

carbonates (sodas), milk and coffee and it continues to be a popular drink            43 with an average consumption of 23 L/y per person. In Europe, the total                44 contribution of the brewing sector to the European economy in terms of                45 [SOURCE: Page_2]

Water consumption may appear to be erratic with ratios 32   varying from 0.3 to 10 L water/L wine, depending on the winery. The ea [SOURCE: Page_3]

33   establishment of regulations and the levying of taxes on winery effluents, the 34   implementation of water purification treatments and the improved awareness 35   of operators in relation to water management have contributed to reducing 36   water consumption to approximately 0.8 L/L (Rochard et al., 1996; ITV, dh [SOURCE: Page_3]

37   2000; Rochard, 2005). 38      Agricultural alcohol may be distilled from many plants that produce either 39   simple sugars directly (cane, beet, sweet sorghum) or starch (corn, grain, 40   sorghum). The distribution, according to Berg (2006), between beverage, o [SOURCE: Page_3]

Brewing, winemaking and distilling: overview of wastewater                  931 [SOURCE: Page_4]

32 000                                                                                  1 [SOURCE: Page_4]

d Fuel 2 28 000         Beverage 3 [SOURCE: Page_4]

ite Industrial 24 000 4 5 Production (106 LPA) [SOURCE: Page_4]

20 000                                                                                  6 [SOURCE: Page_4]

gL 8000                                                                                   11 12 4000                                                                                   13 14 0 1975          1980        1985         1990 hin           1995        2000         2005   15 Year                                        16 17 Fig. 35.1       World alcohol production in million litres of pure alcohol (Berg, 2006).    18 19 spirits organisation (CEPS, 2006), the EU is the leading exporter world-wide                                 20 lis [SOURCE: Page_4]

of spirit drinks. The annual value of EU export is 7 5.4 billion. Spirit drinks                              21 make a positive contribution of 7 4.5 billion to the EU’s balance of trade.                                  22 This contributes 10 % of total EU food and drink exports. It is significantly                                23 ub [SOURCE: Page_4]

larger than the figure for wine exports and more than double the figure for                                  24 beer.                                                                                                        25 Besides the beverage production, the second large market for ethanol is in                                26 industrial applications as solvents that are primarily utilised in the production                            27 dP [SOURCE: Page_4]

of paints, coatings, pharmaceuticals, adhesives inks and other products (≈                                   28 6.5 10 9 LPA/y). Production and consumption is concentrated in the                                           29 industrialised countries in Northern America, Europe and Asia (Berg, 2004).                                  30 The last usage category is fuel alcohol, which is either used in blends or                                   31 pure. Its production has been increasing sharply since 2000 as it was                                        32 approximately 18 109 L/y in 2000, 28 109 L/y in 2004 and is projected to ea [SOURCE: Page_4]

33 reach 60 109 L PA/y in 2010). In 2003, around 61 % of world ethanol production                               34 was produced from sugar crops, be it beet, cane or molasses, while the                                       35 remainder was produced from grains where maize or corn was the main                                          36 dh [SOURCE: Page_4]

feedstock. Leaving aside biomass as a feedstock, the raw material accounts                                   37 for around 70–80 %, of the overall cost of fuel ethanol. Therefore, its relative                             38 abundance plays a crucial role in getting the fuel alcohol industry started in                               39 a particular country. By 2013, fuel ethanol will be produced in North America                                40 o [SOURCE: Page_4]

(the USA and Canada), in South America, Africa, India and Australia from                                     41 cane sugar (juice and molasses) and in the European Union from beet sugar                                    42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_4]

(juice and molasses) and wheat (Berg, 2004). Two main sectors are then                                       43 considered: ethanol from sugar (cane and beet) and ethanol from grain (corn                                  44 and wheat).                                                                                                  45 [SOURCE: Page_4]

932 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_5]

1       The role of environmental technology for industry has greatly evolved [SOURCE: Page_5]

d 2    over the past decades. Since the mid-seventies, the general trend is to consider 3    that pollution from industrial processes should be cleaned up. ‘The polluters [SOURCE: Page_5]

ite 4    pay’ remains the basis of regulations. During beer, wine or alcohol production, 5    the product goes through a whole series of chemical and biochemical reactions 6    (mashing, boiling, fermentation, distillation, evaporation) which require solid– [SOURCE: Page_5]

im 7    liquid separations, cleaning, other water processes and energy. The brewing, 8    wine and alcohol industries have grown from ancient tradition but stand as 9    a dynamic sector open to new technological and scientific developments. 10   These agro-industries recognise that business success should depend upon [SOURCE: Page_5]

gL 11   consumer perception of company reliability. To be considered reliable by 12   consumers, they are making efforts to establish compliance statements, to 13   guarantee the quality of their product, to build consumer satisfaction and 14   confidence, and to actually practice ecoship and sustainability management. 15   Ecoship management can be defined as an attitude and policy towards 16 17 hin environmental issues. The aim is to take advantage of natural energy sources, to promote reuse of packaging and recycling waste, to reduce waste and to 18   promote diversification (Kawasaki and Kondo, 2005). 19      In 1996, the European Union approved the Integrated Pollution Prevention 20   and Control (IPPC) directive 96/61/EC (EC, 1996). The IPPC directive lis 21   constitutes an important tool to identify and quantify the environmental 22   impact of production with life-cycle analysis (LCA), and to define the best 23   available techniques (BAT) under both economically and technically viable ub [SOURCE: Page_5]

24   conditions (CBMC, 2002; Koroneos et al., 2005). 25      Brewers, winemakers and ethanol producers are very concerned that the 26   techniques they use are the best in terms of product quality, cost-effectiveness 27   and environmental impact (Fig. 35.2). Consequently energy consumption, dP [SOURCE: Page_5]

28   water use and wastewater generation constitute real economic opportunities 29   for improvements in the existing process. Our present analysis is designed to 30   highlight the emerging and existing constraints in relation to water and waste 31   management in these industries and to give an overview of resource 32   consumption. The most common treatments and the associated constraints ea [SOURCE: Page_5]

33   and advantages are reported and possible biological and technical alternatives 34   to reduce water consumption and waste production are discussed. Higher 35   efficiencies and tighter environmental restrictions stand as a new framework 36   for environmental technology, in which sustainability and economy are the dh [SOURCE: Page_5]

37   keywords. 38 39 40 o [SOURCE: Page_5]

41   35.2 Water use: the Origin and nature of effluents 42   in the brewing, wine and distilling industries Wo [SOURCE: Page_5]

936 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_9]

1    water is used as rinsing water in the brewing house (during the production) [SOURCE: Page_9]

d 2    and in the bottling plant. In addition to the hot water required for the brewing 3    process (depending on the mashing programme and mash water cycles 0.9– [SOURCE: Page_9]

ite 4    1.1 L/L including false bottom rinsing, product displacement and vessel 5    cleaning), additional quantities of hot water are needed in the plant for 6    cleaning and sterilisation operations. Specific hot process water requirements [SOURCE: Page_9]

im 7    fluctuate widely between 0.2 and 1.5 L/L of cast wort (Schu and Stolz, 8    2005). Cooling and brewing water only comprise a small part of the water 9    consumption: cooling water is usually only needed as supplementation water 10   in a closed circuit; the brewing water is essentially the basis of the end- [SOURCE: Page_9]

gL 11   product (Braeken et al., 2004). 12       In brewing, the average water consumption is correlated to beer production 13   for industrial breweries (Perry and De Villiers, 2003). Water consumption is 14   divided into 2/3 used in the process and 1/3 in the cleaning operations (Moll, 15   1991). In the same way, the effluent to beer ratio is correlated to beer production. 16 17 hin It has been shown that the effluent load is very similar to the water load since none of this water is used to brew beer and most of it ends up as effluent 18   (Perry and De Villiers, 2003). The wastewater discharge will be equal to the 19   water supply minus the beer produced, water evaporated in brew house and 20   utility plants, and the water present in the by-products and solid-wastes lis 21   (spent Kieselguhr, surplus yeast and spent grains). Water loss along the 22   process is estimated to be 1.3–1.8 L water/L beer. 23 (Water/Beer) = 2.89 + 8731200/Beer        For 30 < Beer < 60 106 L/month ub [SOURCE: Page_9]

24 25   (Effluent/Beer) = 2.21 + 54589200/Beer    Beer [L/month] with 26                                             Effluent/Beer and Water/Beer, [L/L beer 27 The brewing process generates a unique, high-strength wastewater as a dP [SOURCE: Page_9]

28 29   by-product. The wastewater typically has a high biochemical oxygen demand 30   (BOD) from the carbohydrates and protein used in brewing beer. The 31   wastewater from the brewery is usually quite warm (over 38 °C). Both these 32   specificities make brewery wastewater an ideal substrate for anaerobic treatment. Anaerobic digestion of brewery wastewater is a proven process ea [SOURCE: Page_9]

33 34   with more than 250 full-scale systems in operation (Totzke, 2005). 35 36   Spent grain dh [SOURCE: Page_9]

37   The mashing process is one of the initial operations in brewing, rendering 38   the malt and cereal grain content soluble in water. After extraction, the spent 39   grains and wort (water with extracted matter) are called mash and need to be 40   separated. The amount of solid in the mash is typically 20–30 % but can o [SOURCE: Page_9]

41   reach 40 %. At present, spent grains, often mixed with yeast surplus and cold 42   break (trub separation after cooling of wort), are sold as ruminant livestock Wo [SOURCE: Page_9]

43   feed with an average profit close to 5 7/t (min: 1 7/t, max: 6 7/t, Knirsch et 44   al., 1999). Anaerobic fermentation can be an attractive alternative to waste 45   disposal since it provides a gain of energy, although the composition of spent grain (Table 35.3) requires a specific degradation process. Brewing, winemaking and distilling: overview of wastewater                  937 [SOURCE: Page_10]

Table 35.3 Composition of spent grains and their ability to degrade (Voigt and            1 [SOURCE: Page_10]

d Sommer, 2005)                                                                             2 3 [SOURCE: Page_10]

ite Ingredient                 Barley malt            Wheat malt            Degradation 4 Protein                    20–22                  20–18                 Easy              5 Fat                        16–18                  15–18                 Easy              6 Starch                     0.8–1.0                0.6–1.0               Easy [SOURCE: Page_10]

im 7 Hemicellulose              25–30                  35–40                 Difficult Cellulose                  18–20                  14–16                 Difficult 8 Lignin                     8–10                   4–7                   Difficult         9 Ash                        3–4                    3–4                   Difficult         10 [SOURCE: Page_10]

gL 11 12 Yeast surplus                                                                             13 Maturation and fermentation tank bottoms constitute another source of sludge              14 estimated at 0.025 kg/L beer. Low-fermentation beer is produced through hin                                           15 two fermentation steps, the primary fermentation being when 90 % of the                   16 fermentable matter is consumed. Rapid cooling of the tank stops this                      17 fermentation and causes the flocculation of insoluble particles and the                   18 sedimentation of yeast. The tank bottom becomes full of yeast and ‘green                  19 beer’. At present, the fermentation tank bottom generates a beer loss of                  20 lis [SOURCE: Page_10]

around 1–2 % of production (Nielsen, 1989; Reed, 1989).                                   21 In brewing, surplus yeast is recovered by natural sedimentation at the end             22 of the second fermentation and maturation. The yeast can be sold to the                   23 ub [SOURCE: Page_10]

animal feed industry. This brewing by-product has dry matter content close                24 to 10 %w/w and generates beer losses (or waste) of between 1.5 and 3 % of                 25 the total volume of produced beer.                                                        26 27 dP [SOURCE: Page_10]

Kieselguhr sludge                                                                         28 Diatomaceous earth has various advantages for filtration in the brewing                   29 process as reported by Baimel et al. (2004). The conventional dead-end                    30 filtration with filter-aids (Kieselguhr) has been the standard industrial practice        31 for more than 100 years and will be increasingly scrutinised from economic,               32 ea [SOURCE: Page_10]

environmental and technical standpoints in the coming century (Hrycyk,                    33 1997; Knirsch et al., 1999). Approximately two-thirds of diatomaceous earth               34 production is used in the beverage industry (beer, wine, fruit juice and liqueurs).       35 The conventional dead-end filtration with filter-aids consumes a large quantity           36 dh [SOURCE: Page_10]

of diatomaceous earth (1–2 g/L of clarified beer) and carries serious                     37 environmental, sanitary and economical implications (Modrok et al., 2006).                38 At the end of the separation process, diatomaceous earth sludge (containing               39 water and organic substances) has more than tripled in weight. From the                   40 o [SOURCE: Page_10]

environmental point of view, the diatomaceous earth is recovered from open-               41 pit mines and constitutes a natural and finite resource. The resources of                 42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_10]

good-quality Kieselguhr are limited and brewers are facing problems with                  43 the continuously increasing iron content of the raw material. After use, recovery,        44 recycling and disposal of Kieselguhr (after filtration) are a major difficulty            45 [SOURCE: Page_10]

Woodhead Publishing Limited; proof copy not for publication [SOURCE: Page_10]

938 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_11]

1    due to its polluting effect and the increasing cost of disposal. From a health [SOURCE: Page_11]

d 2    perspective, the diatomaceous earth is classified as ‘hazardous waste’ before 3    and after filtration (The World Health Organization defines the crystalline [SOURCE: Page_11]

ite 4    silica as a cause of lung disease) and its use requires safe working conditions. 5    From an economic standpoint, the diatomaceous earth consumption and sludge 6    disposal generate the main cost of the filtration process ranging between 0.0025 and 0.007 7/L. In Europe, the economic aspect is strengthened because [SOURCE: Page_11]

im 7 8    its consumption is higher (around 1.7 g/L of clarified beer). The disposal 9    routes of Kieselguhr sludge are into agriculture and recycling with an average 10   cost of 170 7/t. Disposal costs vary widely from one brewery to another [SOURCE: Page_11]

gL 11   with a positive income of 7.5 7/t up to a maximum charge of 1100 7/t of 12   Kieselguhr purchased (Knirsch et al., 1999). 13 14 15   35.2.2 Wine industry 16 17 • hin The wine industry can be divided into two sectors of activity: wine production (winemaking) within the wineries that creates winery 18 effluents and co-products: pomace, lees; 19 •   transformation/recycling of winery co-products within wine distilleries 20 lis (alcohol distillation, extraction of components, etc.), whose wastewaters 21 consist mainly of stillage. 22 23   These two sectors can be differentiated by the highly different production ub [SOURCE: Page_11]

24   processes and raw materials used, leading to different types of effluent produced 25   and treatment and recycling methods specific to each one (Fig. 35.4). 26      One of the main characteristics of winery effluents is linked to the seasonal 27   character of the production with heavy pollution loads discharged over a dP [SOURCE: Page_11]

28   short period of time (grape harvest, winemaking). The transformation of the 29   by-products resulting from wine production (pomace, lees) by distilleries 30   leads to the production of highly polluted wastewater (stillage). 31      The range of methods for treating and eliminating effluent (spreading, 32   biological wastewater treatment, aerobic and anaerobic techniques, heat ea [SOURCE: Page_11]

33   concentration, etc.) was transposed to the wine sector. However, constraints 34   linked to the characteristics of the effluents and the companies involved 35   resulted in the emergence of suitable treatment methods: aerated storage, 36   aerated lagooning, natural evaporation for winery effluents, anaerobic digestion dh [SOURCE: Page_11]

37   for stillage, etc. (OIV, 1999; ITV, 2000). 38 39   Winery wastewater 40   Water use and wastewater o [SOURCE: Page_11]

41   Winery wastewater mainly consists of the water used to wash and clean 42   winery equipment and facilities used for destalking, pressing, racking, alcoholic Wo [SOURCE: Page_11]

43   and malolactic fermentation, clarifying, tartaric stabilisation, filtering and 44   bottling operations. The organic pollution of the effluent is due to the 45   contribution of matter from wash water and product loss. [SOURCE: Page_11]

940 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_13]

1       Wineries vary considerably, in production capacity – from several tens of [SOURCE: Page_13]

d 2    thousands to several tens of millions of litres of wine – and as a result of the 3    extremely varied vinification methods and techniques used. They also vary [SOURCE: Page_13]

ite 4    as to their water resources – underground and/or drinking water systems – as 5    well as to the wastewater treatment methods that they use and to the level of 6    awareness of the operators responsible for water management. All these [SOURCE: Page_13]

im 7    differences lead to water consumption levels that vary considerably from 8    one establishment to another: from 0.3–10 L of water per litre of wine 9    produced (Duarte et al., 1998; Picot and Cabanis, 1998; ITV, 2000). The 10   establishment of regulations and the levying of taxes on winery effluents, the [SOURCE: Page_13]

gL 11   implementation of water purification treatments and the improved awareness 12   of operators in relation to water management have contributed to the reduction 13   of water consumption to approximately 0.8 L/L (Rochard et al., 1996; ITV, 14   2000; Rochard, 2005). The amount of taxes levied on waste depends on the 15   country; the French and Italian wine industries generate six times less effluents 16 17 hin than those of Spain where taxes are lower (Prodanov and Cobo Reuters, 2003; Bustamante et al., 2005). The seasonality of wine production activity 18   is an important factor to be taken into consideration in the management of 19   wastewater treatment. Of the annual volume of effluents 60 % is produced 20   over a period of approximately two months (harvest/vinification) and waste lis 21   production is maximal from the start. 22 23   Water management ub [SOURCE: Page_13]

24   After separating rain water from uncontaminated process water (cooling 25   water), efforts to reduce water consumption focused on washing and cleaning 26   operations, the choice of materials and the intrinsic consumption of the 27   various operations involved. Since the washing of facilities (tanks, equipment, dP [SOURCE: Page_13]

28   floors) is a major source of water consumption in wineries, the use of high- 29   pressure blowers (> 50 bars) or medium-pressure blowers (20–40 bars) that 30   are just as efficient but without the disadvantages (less splattering, aerosols 31   and abrasion), makes it possible to reduce water consumption (Seegers, 32   2006). The nature and the quality of tank construction materials are also ea [SOURCE: Page_13]

33   considered in terms of water management. For example, the use of 34   electropolished-type stainless steel for tanks not only reduces the quantity of 35   water required for washing but the pollution load discharged into the water 36   as well, as a result of decreased adherence and retention of matter on the tank dh [SOURCE: Page_13]

37   surface. Concerning cleaning (disinfecting) of equipment, the application of 38   chemicals (biocides) in the form of foams is recommended to limit product 39   consumption and to increase efficiency. 40       Among the different vinification operations, the filtering of musts and o [SOURCE: Page_13]

41   wines is an important step in the management of water and waste. Membrane 42   filtering processes (tangential microfiltration) applied to wine production Wo [SOURCE: Page_13]

43   are a considerable improvement in terms of the environmental impact of 44   vinification processes (Moutounet and Vernet, 1998). Some of the advantages 45   offered by membrane filtration as opposed to clay filtration are: the suppression [SOURCE: Page_13]

Brewing, winemaking and distilling: overview of wastewater          941 [SOURCE: Page_14]

of filtration waste (using clay), whose elimination is increasingly difficult;      1 [SOURCE: Page_14]

d the decrease in raw material loss (loss of wine through imbibitions), and the       2 reduction of the pollution load in effluents. Nevertheless, water consumption       3 [SOURCE: Page_14]

ite for membrane filtration, linked to cleaning-in-place (CIP) procedures, is not       4 actually less than that of clay filtration (Kerner et al., 2004). Moreover, the     5 substitution of mineral filtration additives with substances suitable for           6 [SOURCE: Page_14]

im reconditioning (Salame et al., 1998) or biodegradation (Erbslöh, 2006)              7 contributes to the improved management of filtration residues.                      8 Tartaric stabilisation of wines is a very specific operation and necessary      9 if the wine is to conform to quality criteria. It is often carried out by cooling   10 [SOURCE: Page_14]

gL the wine at temperatures below freezing (–4 °C) for around eight days, and          11 then filtering it to eliminate potassium acid tartrate precipitates. This process   12 consumes a great deal of electrical energy (5 kWh/m^3 wine) and produces             13 considerable quantities of waste (filtration clay: 2 kg/m^3 wine). Electrodialysis   14 is a new technology used for the tartaric stabilisation of wine (Escudier et hin                                     15 al., 1993) with a better environmental record: energy consumption is greatly        16 reduced (0.5–1 kWh/m^3 ) and filtration wastes are eliminated. Water                 17 consumption in the electodialysis brine circuit (0.1 L/L wine) can be reduced       18 through reverse osmosis (RO) of the brine and by recycling the permeate in          19 the process (Bories et al., 2006).                                                  20 lis [SOURCE: Page_14]

21 Organic load and composition of winery wastewaters                                  22 Studies on winery effluents have generally focused on the evaluation of             23 ub [SOURCE: Page_14]

overall pollution loads on the basis of pollution measurement criteria –            24 chemical oxygen demand (COD), biological oxygen demand (BOD, suspended              25 solids (SS), etc. This research has shown that there is a wide disparity between    26 winery effluents depending on the winery, the activity and the production           27 dP [SOURCE: Page_14]

period. On average, winery effluents have a COD close to 15 g O$_2$/L, and             28 this organic load is easily biodegradable (COD/BOD < 1.5). Low nitrogen             29 and phosphorus contents are observed and contribute to an insufficient BOD/         30 N/P ratio in aerobic biological treatment. The quantity of sugars (glucose          31 and fructose) in musts – 200–250 g/L – and ethanol in wines – 100–120 g/            32 ea [SOURCE: Page_14]

L – that present a similar COD (# 250 g O$_2$/L), contribute to the high organic       33 load of effluents. Cleaning and disinfecting chemicals mainly consisting of         34 caustic soda and biocides (hypochlorite, hydrogen peroxide, quaternary              35 ammonium), very occasionally lead to a high level of alkalinity of the effluents    36 dh [SOURCE: Page_14]

(pH > 10) that are generally acidic (pH 3.5–5).                                     37 The detailed composition and the proportion of the different components          38 of the pollution load of winery effluents have recently been studied (Bories        39 et al., 1998; Colin et al., 2005). Ethanol is the major organic component and       40 o [SOURCE: Page_14]

accounts for up to 90 % of the COD, except during the grape harvest                 41 when it is mainly sugars (Table 35.4). Winery effluents may contain almost          42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_14]

1 % (vol/vol) ethanol, corresponding to a wine diluted ten-fold. A close            43 correlation has been shown between the COD of winery effluent and ethanol           44 content.                                                                            45 [SOURCE: Page_14]

Woodhead Publishing Limited; proof copy not for publication [SOURCE: Page_14]

Brandy production and the 22 distillation of excess wine production generate wine stillage. Taking the 23 alcohol content of co-products into account (5–12 % v/v), the stillage volume ub [SOURCE: Page_15]

24 (dealcoholised product + condensed steam) represents approximately 10– 25 20 L/L of pure alcohol. 26 Water consumption in wine distilleries is obviously linked to the production 27 of steam for distillation and cooling (condensers, exchangers), as well as to dP [SOURCE: Page_15]

28 the extraction of alcohol from the pomace by steeping with water. The recycling 29 of pomace stillage for the extraction of pomace alcohol is used to reduce 30 water consumption. Contrary to wineries whose waste production is 31 concentrated over short periods of time, distillery activity is spread out over 32 a large part of the year as a result of the chronology of the production of co- ea [SOURCE: Page_15]

33 products – pomace, lees, wine – and their storage. 34 35 Load and composition of distillery stillage 36 dh [SOURCE: Page_15]

The dissolved organic components found in stillage are glycerol, organic 37 acids (tartaric, malic/lactic, succinic, acetic) and other wine components 38 (phenolic compounds, nitrogenous matter and polysaccharides). The absence 39 of ethanol in the stillage clearly differentiates it from winery effluent. Three 40 o [SOURCE: Page_15]

types of stillage – lees, pomace and wine – have very distinct characteristics 41 (Table 35.5). 42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_15]

Lees stillage is rich in suspended matter (50–100 g SS/L): yeasts and 43 crystals of potassium hydrogen tartrate, giving it a particularly high raw 44 COD (80–120 g O2/L). Of the dissolved organic matter in detartrated lees 45 stillage (COD d # 30 g O2/L) 45 % is due to simple compounds (glycerol, Woodhead Publishing Limited; proof copy not for publication [SOURCE: Page_15]

Pomace stillage resulting directly from the extraction of alcohol and sugars            24 by washing with water has a COD of 15–20 g O$_2$/L, whereas recycled pomace                    25 wine stillage is characterised by a high organic load (COD: 30–50 g O$_2$/L).                  26 Almost 70 % of the organic load of wine stillage (COD: 20–30 g O$_2$/L)                    27 dP [SOURCE: Page_16]

consists of glycerol and organic acids. The BOD/COD of wine stillage is the                 28 highest (0.44–0.52) and testifies to its satisfactory biodegradability in relation          29 to the high proportion of simple substances.                                                30 Concerning the nitrogen and phosphorus composition, pomace and lees                     31 stillage have BOD/N/P ratios of 100/3.2/2.0 and 100/3.8/1.6, respectively                   32 ea [SOURCE: Page_16]

(Bories, 1978). However, wine stillage is characterised by a ratio of 100/0.6/              33 0.4 that clearly reveals the deficiency in N and P for aerobic biological                   34 treatment.                                                                                  35 Concerning the mineral composition of stillage, potassium is the major                  36 dh [SOURCE: Page_16]

element. It can be very highly concentrated in lees stillage (8–10 g K/L).                  37 Moreover, detartrated lees stillage is rich in sulphate (8–10 g SO$_4$/L) or chloride,         38 depending on the reagents used for the extraction of calcium tartrate: lime/                39 calcium sulphate or lime/calcium chloride.                                                  40 o [SOURCE: Page_16]

944 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_17]

1    listed by the Central Pollution Control Board (Nataraj et al. 2006). For each [SOURCE: Page_17]

d 2    litre of alcohol produced, the molasses-based distilleries would usually have 3    water consumption per litre of alcohol produced of 14–22 L in process [SOURCE: Page_17]

ite 4    applications (yeast propagation, molasses preparation, steam generation) and 5    100–240 L in non-process applications (cooling water, steam generation). 6    They generate about 8–15 L of wastewater. [SOURCE: Page_17]

im 7        In all the schemes, it is possible to distinguish two types of wastewater: 8 •   wastewater with high solids concentrations as spent wash (named also 9 stillage or vinasse) removed from the bottom of the column receiving 10 the fermented broth, [SOURCE: Page_17]

gL 11 •   wastewater with very low solids concentration as cooling water used to 12 evacuate the heat from the fermentation and distillation steps and the 13 condensates from the stillage concentration plants. 14 15   The treatment of the first is very dependent on the raw material used for the 16 17 hin fermentation. In contrast, in the second case their characteristics are the same. 18 19   Spent wash from the distillation column 20   The main difficulty comes from the spent wash issued from the bottom of the lis 21   column receiving the fermented broth. Its composition, treatment and recycling 22   schemes depend on the raw material used to produce the alcohol. The process, 23   with cane and beet sugar products, is nearly the same and reported in Fig. 35.5. ub [SOURCE: Page_17]

24      The distilleries that ferment cane juice produce spent wash with a low 25   concentration of solids (2–4 % solids) but high COD level (14–34 g/L) 26   (Table 35.6) (Decloux and Bories, 2001). Their biodegradability is high 27   (BOD/COD > 0.6) as 87 % of the COD of the cane juice stillage is represented dP [SOURCE: Page_17]

28   by simple compounds: glycerol, organic acids. The glycerol alone represents 29   38 %. Direct land application, anaerobic biodigestion, aerobic treatment and 30   discharge in aquatic environments are the main post-treatments. 31      Fermentation units working with cane molasses or green cane syrup need 32   dilution water to decrease the sugar concentration to 16 % before fermentation. ea [SOURCE: Page_17]

33   They produce stillage (8–10 % solids) of variable chemical composition 34   with high mineral and organic matter content. Its COD is between 60 and 35   120 g/L. The BOD/COD ratio (0.3–0.35) demonstrates the limited 36   biodegradability of the organic load. Substances which are not easily dh [SOURCE: Page_17]

37   biodegradable represent a large proportion of the COD. It is made up of 38   complex compounds (hetero-polymers) responsible for the dark brown colour 39   of molasses stillage (phenolic compounds, mixtures of caramels, melanoidins 40   and products of the alkaline degradation of hexoses). The mineral load is o [SOURCE: Page_17]

41   mainly made up of potassium (4–12 g/L), magnesium (2–3 g/L), calcium 42   (2–3 g/L) sulphate (4–8 g/L) and chloride (5–6 g/L). Cane molasses stillage Wo [SOURCE: Page_17]

43   is rich in glycerol. Direct land application, anaerobic digestion, aerobic 44   treatment, livestock feed production and other forms of recycling are the 45   main post-treatments. [SOURCE: Page_17]

Woodhead Publishing Limited; proof copy not for publication [SOURCE: Page_17]

gL 11   Fixed matter              (g/L)        3.7               21.5            12.7 12   Nitrogen (N)              (g/L)        0.15–0.70          0.45–5.18       0.48–0.70 Phosphorus (P2O$_5$)         (g/L)        0.01–0.21          0.10–0.29       0.09–0.20 13   Potassium (K2O)           (g/L)        1.2–2.1            3.74–7.83       3.34–4.60 14   Calcium (CaO)             (g/L)        0.13–1.54          0.45–5.18       1.33–4.57 15   Magnesium (MgO)           (g/L)        0.2–0.49           0.42–1.52       0.58–0.70 16 17 Sulfate (SO$_4$) Carbon (C) C/N ratio (g/L) (g/L) hin 0.60–0.76 5.7–13.4 19.7–21.07 6.4 11.2–22.9 16–16.27 3.7 8.7–12.1 16.4 18 19   BOD = biological oxygen demand 20   COD = chemical oxygen demand lis 21 22      Almost all the distilleries using beet juice are located alongside a sugar 23   beet factory. The spent wash is recycled into the beet diffuser. Outside the ub [SOURCE: Page_19]

24   beet harvest period, distilleries produce alcohol principally from molasses, 25   green syrup (intermediate crystallisation products) or sugar syrup. As the 26   total dissolved solids of the raw material is around 75 %, a mixture of water 27   and backset stillage is used to dilute the broth to about 16 % sugar before dP [SOURCE: Page_19]

28   fermentation. The amount of backset stillage is limited by the increasing 29   osmotic pressure induced. The excess must be treated. Stillage from beet 30   molasses fermentation has an acid pH, a dry matter content of about 100 g/ 31   L including 60 % of organic matter, a COD of around 60 g/L and a BOD of 32   about 30 g/L (Table 35.7). The potassium content (K2O) is high (8 g/ ea [SOURCE: Page_19]

33   100 g solids) as well as the glycerol (6 g/L) and betaine (15–20 g/L). The 34   main utilisation is to concentrate it to produce liquid fertiliser (syrup with 35   55 % solids) with, in certain cases, an extraction of potassium sulphate crystals. 36   Other forms of recycling are in study. The main cereals used to produce dh [SOURCE: Page_19]

37   ethanol are maize in the USA and wheat in Europe and Australia. The general 38   process in represented in Fig. 35.6. 39      There are two main production processes differentiated by the initial 40   treatment of the grain. In the first one, the whole grain is used to produce the o [SOURCE: Page_19]

41   mash: the entire corn kernel or other starch grain is first ground into flour 42   and processed without separating out the various component parts of the Wo [SOURCE: Page_19]

43   grain. Water is added to form a ‘mash’. This slurry is then treated with a 44   liquefying enzyme called α-amylase to hydrolyse the cereal to dextrins, 45   which are a mix of oligosaccharides. The hydrolysis is done above the [SOURCE: Page_19]

im 7 Glycerine                  9.0            13.6         26.0 Betaine                   14.4            14.2         12.8 8 Sodium                     2.0             2.4          0.9               9 Potassium (K2O)            7.7            10.7          8.8               10 [SOURCE: Page_20]

gL Sulfate                    1.2             1.0          1.5               11 Chloride                   1.0             4.5          3.8               12 Calcium                    0.08            0.3          0.2 Magnesium                  0.03            0.02         0.03 13 Nitrogen (N)               4.2             4.04         2.9               14 TOC                       37.0    hin     36.88        42.4               15 16 TOC = total organic compounds 17 18 temperature of gelatinisation of the cereal by cooking the mash at an appropriate   19 temperature to break down the granular structure of the starch. The dextrins        20 lis [SOURCE: Page_20]

are further hydrolysed to glucose in the saccharification process using the         21 exo-enzyme glucoamylase. Then the mash is cooled and transferred to                 22 fermenters where yeast is added. After fermentation, the resulting ‘beer’ is        23 ub [SOURCE: Page_20]

transferred to distillation columns where the ethanol is separated. The stillage    24 extracted at the bottom of the column is sent through a centrifuge that separates   25 the coarse grain from the solubles that are then concentrated to about 30 %         26 solids by evaporation, resulting in condensed distillers solubles (CDS) or          27 dP [SOURCE: Page_20]

‘syrup’. The coarse grain and the syrup are then dried together to produce          28 dried distillers grains solubles (DDGS), a high-quality and nutritious livestock    29 feed. Most of the new corn distilleries use this process or a minor variation       30 of it. In the second process, the different parts of the grain are separated        31 before hydrolysis of the starch. For the wheat, the separation process is the       32 ea [SOURCE: Page_20]

same to produce the flour, and then the fibre and the gluten are removed and        33 processed separately. The advantage of this process is a better recycling           34 value of the co-products and easier fermentation, but the disadvantage is a         35 drop in the yield as the recovery of the starch is not complete.                    36 dh [SOURCE: Page_20]

For maize the grain needs to be soaked or ‘steeped’ in water and dilute          37 sulphurous acid for 24 to 48 hours to facilitate the separation of the grain        38 into its many component parts. After steeping the grain slurry is processed         39 through a series of grinders to separate the germ. The remaining fibre, gluten      40 o [SOURCE: Page_20]

and starch components are further segregated using centrifugal, screen and          41 hydroclonic separators. The steeping liquor is concentrated in an evaporator        42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_20]

and co-dried with the fibre component. It is then sent to the livestock industry.   43 The gluten component (protein) is filtered off and dried to produce the             44 gluten meal co-product. This process requires large volumes of water (1.3 m^3/       45 [SOURCE: Page_20]

d starch and any remaining water from the mash can then be processed in one        2 of three ways: fermented to ethanol, dried and sold as dried or modified corn    3 [SOURCE: Page_22]

ite starch, or processed into corn syrup. The fermentation process for ethanol is    4 very similar to the cane or beet juice process described previously.             5 In the USA, most fuel ethanol is produced from maize following either         6 [SOURCE: Page_22]

im the dry-grind (67 %) or the wet-mill (33 %) process. Theoretically, 1 kg of      7 corn can yield a maximum of 0.44 LPA. Realistically, however, a yield of         8 between 0.37 and 0.41 LPA/kg is common, although the newest plants can           9 achieve up to 0.42 LPA/kg (Rosentrater and Kuthukumarappan, 2006). The           10 [SOURCE: Page_22]

gL production of DDGS is 0.30 kg/kg maize. The chemical properties of maize         11 distillers dried grains with solubles were reviewed by Rosentrater and           12 Kuthukumarappan (2006) and are reported in Table 35.8.                           13 Until now, recycling of the co-products was mainly in the form of livestock   14 feeds as DDGS. A potential market exists in the world’s animal feed industry hin                                  15 where traditionally-used sources of protein such as animal by-products and       16 fish meal have been either eliminated due to concern surrounding mad cow         17 disease (BSE) or have becomed less available and more costly. The combined       18 protein and energy value of ethanol by-products gives them tremendous            19 potential in animal feeds across the world. Nevertheless, research is being      20 lis [SOURCE: Page_22]

continued to find better reuse opportunities and the bio-refinery concept        21 where the parameters are chosen not only for the ethanol production but also     22 for the valorisation of the co-product is generally accepted. (Dawson, 2003).    23 ub [SOURCE: Page_22]

As new technologies are implemented, adding value to co-products is           24 essential to the profitability of the fuel business. This will require a more    25 holistic approach to ethanol in dry-grind plants. Optimisation of co-products    26 as well as ethanol yield must be considered.                                     27 dP [SOURCE: Page_22]

950 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_23]

1    and from the top of the distillation columns. To prevent any decrease in [SOURCE: Page_23]

d 2    fermentation kinetics, water cooler than 30 °C is necessary. In the condensers 3    of the distillation columns, the cooling water temperature must be lowered [SOURCE: Page_23]

ite 4    as the distillation pressure is lowered, but generally not lower than 45 °C. 5    The dehydratation step also needs cooling water at about 50 °C. 6        Some small distilleries may be on the coast or near a river and use the [SOURCE: Page_23]

im 7    cooling water in an open loop (pumping in cold water and sending the heated 8    water back into the sea or river). However, with the increasingly stringent 9    environmental rules, this scheme is less and less used. The distillery plants 10   must have a cooling system to recycle the water. Most of them use an air- [SOURCE: Page_23]

gL 11   cooling exchanger. This implies evaporation of water into the air and hence 12   the necessity to replace it with fresh water. Furthermore, to prevent salt 13   accumulation in this cooling loop, a small flux of water must be regularly 14   removed from the system. Thus a consumption of fresh water is necessary to 15   ensure heat removal from the distillation columns. The main problem of this 16 17 hin circuit is not the water consumption or the water quality even if disinfectant treatments are needed to prevent bacterial development, but the difficulty in 18   reaching sufficiently low temperatures, in particular in warm countries with 19   humid air. In some very large fuel plants an electrical cooling system may be 20   the solution. lis 21       The concentration of stillage by evaporation generates large volumes of 22   condensate which cannot be discarded without treatment because of its COD 23   which ranges from 1–10 g/L (Morin et al., 2003). It is mainly used as water ub [SOURCE: Page_23]

24   for irrigation. However, tight regulations make this utilisation has easy than 25   it looks. Furthermore, alcoholic fermentation requires a major input of water. 26   Some treatments are in study to allow the recycling of this water in fermenters 27   are being studied. dP [SOURCE: Page_23]

28 29 30 31   35.3 Most widely used treatment methods: Livestock feed, 32   discharge, anaerobic and aerobic treatments, incineration ea [SOURCE: Page_23]

33 34   Several techniques can be considered as existing industrial practice, but 35   livestock feed, discharge in soil, and biological (aerobic and anaerobic) 36   treatments stand as the most widely used. The specificity of the brewing, dh [SOURCE: Page_23]

37   winemaking and distilling industries leads to different levels of development 38   for each technique (Table 35.9). Their levels of development, advantages 39   and constraints are reported. The choice of wastewater treatment techniques 40   is based on numerous parameters: o [SOURCE: Page_23]

41   •   knowledge of process and product specificities; 42   •   characterisation of the effluent (nature, composition, concentration, Wo [SOURCE: Page_23]

43       flowrate); 44   •   historical, economical and environmental constraints; 45   •   efficiency of the technique in agreement with BAT selection. [SOURCE: Page_23]

Woodhead Publishing Limited; proof copy not for publication [SOURCE: Page_23]

952 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_25]

1       In the brewing and distilling industries, the most common treatment is [SOURCE: Page_25]

d 2    livestock feed, discharge in soil and biological treatment. In wine production, 3    the choice of wastewater treatment techniques is based on the seasonal nature [SOURCE: Page_25]

ite 4    and dependent on winery production capacities. Spreading and natural 5    evaporation were among the first treatments to be implemented since they 6    suit the variability of the pollution load and the technical and economic [SOURCE: Page_25]

im 7    context of the sector (limited operating costs and investment). With the 8    development of biological wastewater treatment technologies, companies 9    with large production capacities (distilleries, wineries) began using aerobic 10   and anaerobic biological processes. The specificities of the composition of [SOURCE: Page_25]

gL 11   winery effluents were also a determinant factor in the study and development 12   of new treatment methods (fractionation, membrane separation) and valorisation 13   (molecule production/extraction). 14 15 16 17 35.3.1 Livestock feed                       hin In breweries, the livestock feed is limited by several economical, technological 18   and biological constraints. The fibre content of spent grain is 150–160 g/ 19   kg DM (dry matter) making them unsuitable as a feed for non-ruminant 20   animals (pork, poultry). Spent grain is bulky, due to its high water content lis 21   (70–80 % w/w) making handling and transport inefficient. In wet form the 22   spent grain is not stable and must be consumed within two or three days 23   otherwise a biological degradation takes place. The production of spent grain ub [SOURCE: Page_25]

24   is high in summer when the demand for cattle feed is low, and in certain 25   areas (Africa, Singapore) the cattle feed market does not exist. 26      Heineken Technical Service and 2B Biorefineries (Bruijn et al., 2001; 27   Schwencke, 2006) adapted a grass separation method for use with spent dP [SOURCE: Page_25]

28   grains, which is environmentally sustainable, applicable world-wide and 29   economically viable. The process separates spent grains into two useful 30   fractions, a ‘protein concentrate’ and a ‘fibre concentrate’ and produces a 31   wastewater stream. Wet spent grains (0.18–0.2 kg/L beer with 20–25 % w/ 32   w DM) are collected in a tank from which it is measured into an impeller mixed tank. Water (0.54–0.80 kg/L beer) at 80 °C is added to obtain a ea [SOURCE: Page_25]

33 34   suspension of 5 % w/w DM. This suspension is pumped through a vibrating 35   screen, which separates water and small particles (70 % vol/vol with 1.5– 36   2 % w/w DM) and the coarse material (30 % vol/vol with 16–18 % w/w DM). dh [SOURCE: Page_25]

37   After separation, the fibre material is fed into a screw press for water removal 38   to reach 40 % w/w DM (0.095–0.140 kg/L beer), and the protein fraction is 39   fed into a scroll-type decanter, where it can be dehydrated to 30 % w/w DM 40   (0.030–0.050 kg/L beer). The liquid stream coming from the screw press o [SOURCE: Page_25]

41   and the decanter is recirculated to the mixing tank and extracted as drain 42   (0.60–0.85 L/L beer with an estimated COD 0.02 kg/L). In 2001, the protein Wo [SOURCE: Page_25]

43   concentrate fetched 170 7/t (88 % w/w DM) and the fibre concentrate, 20 7/ 44   t (45 % w/w). The value of the protein product is the most important factor 45   and determines the success and applicability of a spent grain separation [SOURCE: Page_25]

Woodhead Publishing Limited; proof copy not for publication [SOURCE: Page_25]

Brewing, winemaking and distilling: overview of wastewater           953 [SOURCE: Page_26]

process. Three scenarios can be investigated: (i) direct cattle feed with spent      1 [SOURCE: Page_26]

d grain; (ii) press and burn all spent grain without separation; (iii) separate        2 spent grain, combust the fibres (see Section 35..3.5) and sell the proteins as       3 [SOURCE: Page_26]

ite wet product (30 % DM) or spray-dried (80 % DM). Schwencke (2006) reports             4 promising results with nutritional trials of 180 piglets (diet with 30 % protein     5 coming from spent grains). The protein content could be included in diet             6 [SOURCE: Page_26]

im formulation with a net and metabolisable energy value of 117 and 18.0 MJ/            7 kg DM, respectively.                                                                 8 At an experimental level, the incorporation of brewery waste (spent grain)        9 into fish-feed (carp) was investigated by Kaur and Saxena (2004) in India.           10 [SOURCE: Page_26]

gL The better growth performance in fish fed on diets containing brewery waste          11 is attributed to the availability of good-quality protein, as the waste contains     12 more essential amino acids such as lysine, arginine and methionine than fish         13 meal and about three times the level of these amino acids present in rice            14 bran. In beet and cane molasses alcohol production, because of the high salt, hin                                      15 particularly potassium, content stillage used in ruminants is limited to 10 %        16 of the diet to avoid laxative effects (Decloux and Bories, 2002; Nguyen,             17 2003).                                                                               18 In contrast, for cereal alcohol production, cattle feed is the main utilisation   19 of DDGS. Over the years, numerous research studies have been conducted in            20 lis [SOURCE: Page_26]

order to optimise their use in feed rations and, as reported by Rosentrater and      21 Kuthukumarappan (2006), these studies have been comprehensively reviewed             22 by Aines et al. (1986) and UMN (2006). However, today’s DDGS feed                    23 ub [SOURCE: Page_26]

customers are asking for more information than the traditional moisture,             24 protein, fat and fiber analyses. Animal nutritionists want complete nutrient         25 profiles of the ingredients and they want to know the variability of these           26 nutrients as well as the ability to select nutrients they need. Research projects    27 dP [SOURCE: Page_26]

are underway that would modify the amino acid composition, protein                   28 composition or phosphors content of DDGS. DDGS market expansion beyond               29 cattle to swine, poultry and aquaculture is dependent on improving the quality       30 and consistency of the DDGS coproduct.                                               31 32 ea [SOURCE: Page_26]

33 35.3.2 Discharge in soil or ground water                                             34 Most of the effluents from various industrial sources were usually discharged        35 directly in the soil or in ground water. However, this possibility is decreasing     36 dh [SOURCE: Page_26]

due to stringent environmental restrictions. World-wide scarcity of water is         37 another incentive for recovering pure water from such industrial effluents.          38 39 Direct land application                                                              40 o [SOURCE: Page_26]

In the brewing industry, spent grain can be dumped; however, in addition to          41 restrictions or expense, an economical and ecologically feasible solution is         42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_26]

required. Legal restriction for landfill materials such as maximum organic           43 carbon content of 5 % strengthens these limitations. The spreading                   44 characteristics of winery effluents are linked to the C/N (carbon/nitrogen)          45 [SOURCE: Page_26]

954 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_27]

1    ratio; this is generally very high and can result at any given moment in a [SOURCE: Page_27]

d 2    considerable mobilisation of nitrogen in the soil, with a heavy organic load 3    that can precipitate aerobic and anaerobic phenomena capable of leading to [SOURCE: Page_27]

ite 4    the release of calcium, magnesium, iron and manganese, as well as to a 5    concentration in heavy metals (Debroux et al., 2004; Peres et al., 2004; 6    Bustamante et al., 2005). [SOURCE: Page_27]

im 7        The practice of fusing cane or beet distillery stillage for spray irrigation 8    is long established by ethanol production units. Nguyen (2003) highlights 9    the way is which it is trucked as far as economically possible to spray 10   irrigate on cane and beet plantations. The practice varies with the raw material [SOURCE: Page_27]

gL 11   (cane juice or molasses) and the country. The advantages of direct return 12   include formation of an initial buffer to the soil with calcium and magnesium, 13   and improved soil physical properties, increased water and salt retention 14   capacity and an increased soil microflora population. The disadvantages 15   include problems of strong smell, insect invasion, possible increase in soil 16 17 hin acidity, salt leaching and putrefaction. Another reported problem is the buildup of sulphates. These sulphates are reduced in the soil to hydrogen sulphide 18   (bad odour), which is then oxidised into sulphuric acid by sulphur bacteria 19   in the soil. Mahimairaja and Bolan (2004) demonstrated that in India spent 20   wash application at doses higher than 250 m^3/ha is detrimental to crop growth lis 21   and soil fertility, but its use at lower doses (250 m^3/ha) remarkably improves 22   germination, growth and yield of dryland crops. 23       As far as molasses stillage is concerned, direct land application of spent ub [SOURCE: Page_27]

24   wash from molasses fermentation is no longer carried out in Europe. The law 25   distinguishes between categories of effluent depending on the C/N ratio 26   (Decloux and Bories, 2001). Indeed all nitrogen fertiliser of organic origin is 27   mineralised at varying rates depending on the presence or absence of mineral dP [SOURCE: Page_27]

28   nitrogen (essentially ammonium) and organic nitrogen close to mineral nitrogen 29   (urea, uric acid). The C/N ratio is the main factor of evolution since it 30   conditions the mineralisation rate. The volumes and possible periods of land 31   application are not the same depending on the category of effluent. 32 ea [SOURCE: Page_27]

33   Concentration and land application as fertiliser 34   Industrial waste from breweries, especially of organic origin, has a high 35   potential for several agricultural uses as reported in numerous works on 36   laboratory (in vitro or in vivo) or industrial scales. Firs, the use of brewery dh [SOURCE: Page_27]

37   wastes in arid or semi-arid regions, where the organic matter content of soils 38   is rather low, may contribute to reducing environmental problems and enriching 39   the soil. Second, soil-less substrates are used in horticulture for growing 40   seedlings, plant propagation, vegetable production and the production of o [SOURCE: Page_27]

41   ornamental plants in pots; brewery wastes could be used as compost. Third, 42   spent grains and yeast extracts are a source of complex carbohydrates that Wo [SOURCE: Page_27]

43   may have biological activity in order to fortify plants or stave off disease 44   with various reported rates of success. 45       In Turkey, Kütük et al. (2003) investigated the effects of beer factory [SOURCE: Page_27]

Brewing, winemaking and distilling: overview of wastewater          955 [SOURCE: Page_28]

sludge (BFS) mixed with soil on soil properties and sugar beet growth.              1 [SOURCE: Page_28]

d Increasing doses of brewery sludge has a significant effect on the vegetative       2 growth of sugar beet plants. However, the effect of BFS on leaf growth was          3 [SOURCE: Page_28]

ite more pronounced than on root growth. The best application level seems to be         4 10 t ha–1 considering root growth, this being the economic part of the sugar        5 plant. Application above 10 t ha–1 negatively affected the root quality, possibly   6 [SOURCE: Page_28]

im due to high levels of organic acids, NH +4 –N and NO$_3$–N, all released during        7 mineralisation. BSF should be applied to the soil over six or seven months.         8 Garcia-Gomez et al. (2002) evaluated the use of compost (mixture of BFS         9 (yeast and malt), 2.5 % and lemon tree prunings, 97.5 %) in the preparation         10 [SOURCE: Page_28]

gL of substrates for ornamental plants in pots, as peat substitutes and as an          11 alternative to commercial composts used as substrates, and to determine any         12 limitation to their use. Substrates were prepared by combining each compost         13 with Sphagnum peat (p) or commercial substrates (CS) in different proportion        14 (0, 25, 50 and 75 %). The authors demonstrate that compost of agro-industrial hin                                     15 origin can be used for growing ornamental plants, provided the mixture              16 contains at least 25 % peat or CS (up to 75 % with peat and 50 % with CS            17 for calendula, and up to 50 % with peat or CS for calceolaria).                     18 Rogers et al. (2001) studied the effects of formulations based on yeast         19 fractions, spent grains fractions and hops extract, on commercial turf, growth      20 lis [SOURCE: Page_28]

and health. Liquid and dry BioTurf were composed of soluble and particulate         21 fractions from spent grains, combined with yeast extract and glucan, and            22 between 3 and 6 kg/100 m^2 were applied in agricultural field trials. In all         23 ub [SOURCE: Page_28]

cases, BioTurf improved the visual appearance, the rate of growth and the           24 resistance to disease. The biological components can provide basic nutrition        25 in the form of N, P and K and are particularly active in restricting the growth     26 of plant fungal pathogens, Microdochium, Rhizoctonia and Fusarium species.          27 dP [SOURCE: Page_28]

In the beet molasses industry, concentrated beet stillage is mainly used as     28 fertiliser. Researches have demonstrated the fertiliser value of stillage which     29 is classified as an NPK fertiliser. These fertilisers must contain more than 10     30 % of (N + P2O$_5$ + K2O) with a minimum of 3 % nitrogen and 6 % potash                 31 (K2O) and not contain more than 2 % chlorine. The nitrogen of stillage is           32 ea [SOURCE: Page_28]

almost totally in organic form: amino acids, glutamic acid salts, betaine (2–       33 4 %). Fertilising sugar beet with concentrated beet stillage improves the           34 yield per hectare. Beet molasses stillage enjoys a particular status since it is    35 a natural fertiliser produced on a large scale and whose quality is acknowledged    36 dh [SOURCE: Page_28]

unanimously. Concentrated beet stillage can also be used in organic farming         37 in conformity with the European directive CEE 2092/91. The stillage is              38 concentrated at the output of the distillation column in multiple effect            39 evaporators to 55 % solids. The final dry matter content is limited by the          40 o [SOURCE: Page_28]

risks of spontaneous crystallisation of the potassium sulphate and the deposit      41 at the bottom of the storage reservoirs. However, the application of concentrated   42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_28]

stillage cannot be made on all types of land and it requires a concentration        43 phase which is accompanied by a production of condensates with a COD (1–            44 10 g/L) above the discharge norm. These condensates are most often treated          45 [SOURCE: Page_28]

956 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_29]

1    in lagooning or in aeration ponds. Research is being conducted on their [SOURCE: Page_29]

d 2    treatment to enable their recycling in fermentation (see later). 3        As explained by Decloux and Bories (2002) during the concentration of [SOURCE: Page_29]

ite 4    beet molasses stillage, large quantities of potassium tend to crystallise and 5    clog up the evaporators. To limit this spontaneous formation of potassium 6    sulphate crystals during the concentration stage or during the storage of the [SOURCE: Page_29]

im 7    concentrated stillage, many distilleries acidify the fermentation must with 8    hydrochloric acid instead of sulphuric acid, potassium chloride being much 9    more soluble than potassium sulphate. It is, however, possible to promote 10   and control the crystallisation of potassium sulphate that is then used as [SOURCE: Page_29]

gL 11   fertiliser. Moreover, potassium sulphate crystallisation is a legal requirement 12   when using stillage for cattle feed. It must in this case contain less than 2– 13   3 % of potassium per unit dry matter and have a total nitrogen content 14   (measured by mineralisation and multiplied by 6.25) at least equal to 39 %. 15   To obtain complete precipitation of potassium and be within the acceptable 16 17 hin limits for using stillage in cattle feed, it is necessary to add sulphate ions, most of time ammonium sulphate (NH$_4$)2SO$_4$ that increase the total nitrogen 18   content of the concentrated stillage. The cost of the ammonium sulphate is 19   thus in part compensated by a better utilisation of the stillage. Few beet 20   molasses distilleries go as far as to crystallise out the potassium sulphate. On lis 21   the other hand, several distilleries do extract crystals from concentrated 22   stillage, but only to avoid deposits in the storage reservoir. 23 ub [SOURCE: Page_29]

24   Composting 25   To integrate stillage into compost it is necessary to have solid matter available. 26   In the case of wine distilleries, the stillage can be mixed with the grape. In 27   the case of cane alcohol industry, Liu et al. (1995) have shown the utility of dP [SOURCE: Page_29]

28   compost composed of stillage and bagasse. A technique of inoculating the 29   stillage has been developed by Alfa-Laval. The sugar-distillery Yestwant in 30   the Maharastra in India mixes the cooled stillage with a foam (flocculate 31   resulting from the purification by sulphitation and filtration in the presence 32   of bacilli) then inoculates the mixture with bacteria and fungi. It is then ea [SOURCE: Page_29]

33   spread over a large surface in the sun to dry. The compost is regularly (once 34   a week) turned over for aeration with a specially designed machine with a 35   large capacity (> 1000 m^3/h). The total duration of composting is 11 weeks. 36   All the stillage is treated before the rainy season. The compost is a much dh [SOURCE: Page_29]

37   sought fertiliser. More often, press mud generated from the sugar mill is 38   simply mixed with distillery effluent (Nagaraj and Kumar, 2006). 39       In the beet industry, according to Madejon et al. (2001), direct application 40   of concentrated stillage on agricultural land may lead to economical and o [SOURCE: Page_29]

41   environmental problems due to high salinity, low P content and high density. 42   Then composting of stillage with other solid agricultural residues would be Wo [SOURCE: Page_29]

43   used to overcome these disadvantages by producing compost that is easily 44   handed, with higher potassium content and lower salinity. 45 [SOURCE: Page_29]

Brewing, winemaking and distilling: overview of wastewater          957 [SOURCE: Page_30]

Direct dumping in ground water and sea                                              1 [SOURCE: Page_30]

d Some cane juice distilleries send their stillage into the sea at more or less       2 depth. In the French West Indies theses discharges led to unacceptable problems     3 [SOURCE: Page_30]

ite of pollution on the coast and most distilleries have had to build a biological      4 treatment plant.                                                                    5 6 [SOURCE: Page_30]

im Evaporation in ponds                                                                7 Natural evaporation of winery effluents is a relatively simple treatment            8 technique that has been developed in regions with temperate and dry climates,       9 particularly in the south of France, where approximately 180 ponds exist in         10 [SOURCE: Page_30]

gL the largest wine producing region (Languedoc Roussillon: 1.6 109 L wine).           11 Effluents are stored in water-tight ponds (clay) until total evaporation, where     12 the height of the water is determined by the difference between the evaporation     13 capacity and the rainfall. This treatment technique is in agreement with the        14 aims of sustainable development (no consumption of fossil fuel, evaporation hin                                     15 via wind and sun). Storage in evaporation ponds is not subject to variations        16 in flow or pollution load, a major advantage for the treatment of winery            17 effluents. The evaporating capacity can be improved using accelerated               18 evaporation by splashing the wastewater on supports with a large surface            19 area (Duarte and Neto, 1996; Stock and Capelle, 1998).                              20 lis [SOURCE: Page_30]

The main disadvantage is the risk of noxious odours due to the fermentation      21 and transformation of organic matter into volatile fatty acids (VFA) and            22 other volatile compounds (Guillot et al., 2000; Desauziers et al., 2002; Bories,    23 ub [SOURCE: Page_30]

2005). Nevertheless, the formation of foul-smelling compounds can be                24 prevented by the addition of nitrate and the use of anaerobic respiration           25 (denitrification) for the degradation of carbon compounds (Bories, 2005).           26 Generally speaking, the problem of noxious odours linked to effluents            27 dP [SOURCE: Page_30]

(storage, treatment, etc.) is becoming increasingly important in the agrifood       28 sector (Paillier, 2005). Preventive treatments such as the inhibition of            29 fermentation with biocides or nitrate, or curative treatments such as degradation   30 or neutralisation of foul-smelling compounds, as well as the modification of        31 processes (elimination of sulphate in distilleries), have been particularly         32 ea [SOURCE: Page_30]

studied for the wine and oil industries (Le Verge and Bories, 2004; Bories,         33 2006; Chrobak and Ryder, 2006).                                                     34 35 36 dh [SOURCE: Page_30]

35.3.3 Anaerobic digestion                                                          37 Anaerobic treatment is an accepted practice, and various high-output anaerobic      38 reactor designs have been tested at the pilot scale and under fully-operational     39 conditions. The use of this process is increasing on a daily basis.                 40 o [SOURCE: Page_30]

The brewing industry has been at the origin of one BAT in particular, that       41 of anaerobic technology. The anaerobic microbial conversion of organic              42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_30]

matter into biogas is state-of-the-art at this time. Wastewater with a high         43 organic load is preferably treated using anaerobic digestion, for example           44 waste and wastewater produced by the food industry. The fact that anaerobic         45 [SOURCE: Page_30]

958 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_31]

1    treatment systems produce biological sludge at a low rate is a key factor, in [SOURCE: Page_31]

d 2    addition to their ability to reduce chemical and biological oxygen demand 3    (COD, BOD) without energy consumption. The biological treatment of brewery [SOURCE: Page_31]

ite 4    effluents is not really complex and the anaerobic processes used and related 5    performance aspects are well understood and described in the literature. 6    Compact wastewater treatment systems able to produce high-quality effluents [SOURCE: Page_31]

im 7    and to handle nutrient removal are of major industrial interest. However, 8    wastewater from breweries is highly variable (Table 35.10); depending on 9    the step of the brewing process, pH, temperature, quantity, organic load, 10   solids contents, cleaning and disinfecting agents can all change. Volumetric [SOURCE: Page_31]

gL 11   conversion capacities of the biological reactor are defined by (i) the biomass 12   conversion capacity (bacterial kinetic parameters, physicochemical 13   environment), (ii) mass transport (hydrodynamics, reactor geometry) and 14   (iii) biomass concentration (retention of biomass, settler system, viscosity). 15       Considering the heavy organic load of distillery wastewater, anaerobic 16 17 hin digestion has long been considered to be ideal technique, combining the advantages of being both a primary treatment for depolluting the organic 18   load and energy-producing due to the large production of biogas reusable for 19   distillation (Bories and Maugenet, 1978; Chabas et al., 1990). Now used 20   principally for the treatment of industrial liquid effluents, it has been the lis 21   subject of numerous studies in France since the 1980s. Its efficiency for 22   treating carbon pollution has aroused particular interest in the agrifood and 23   pulp industries (Perillat and Boulenger, 2000). Approximately 50 units operate ub [SOURCE: Page_31]

24   in France in the agrifood industry at this time; they are most prevalent in 25   brewing and malting industries, wine distilleries and wineries. However, the 26 27 dP [SOURCE: Page_31]

33                 Total COD                 3.7–22.4 g/L beer 34                                           0.9–4 (max 5.5) g/L 35                 COD/BOD                   1.5–1.8 Total nitrogen           25–85 mg/L 36 dh [SOURCE: Page_31]

Total phosphate           5–35 mg/L 37                 Soluble COD               4.7 g/L 38                 Soluble BOD               3.0 g/L 39                 Total SS                  0.74–2.92 g/L beer 40                                           0.6 g/L o [SOURCE: Page_31]

FOG                       0.05 g/L 41                 Settling sediment         0.15–1.5 g/L 42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_31]

Brewing, winemaking and distilling: overview of wastewater           959 [SOURCE: Page_32]

wide disparity in the composition and production conditions between different        1 [SOURCE: Page_32]

d distillery stillage makes it difficult to generalise about the different data        2 available in each of the sectors. For example, effluents from a cane distillery      3 [SOURCE: Page_32]

ite may have a high BOD/COD, which would lead to the destruction by micro-               4 organisms that are useful in biodegradation. In their efforts to conform to the      5 discharge standards, Indian distilleries use various forms of primary, secondary     6 [SOURCE: Page_32]

im and tertiary treatment. The typical treatment sequence is screening or               7 equalisation, followed by biomethanisation. The biomethanisation effluent            8 is occasionally subjected to a single- or two-stage aerobic treatment using          9 activated sludge, trickling filters or even a second stage of anaerobic treatment    10 [SOURCE: Page_32]

gL in lagoons.                                                                          11 12 Digestion conditions                                                                 13 The anaerobic digestion process includes several microbiological stages to           14 transform the organic matter: (i) a hydrolysis phase of complex substrates hin                                      15 (polysaccharides, proteins) using hydrolytic bacteria, (ii) a fermentation stage     16 to convert simple substrates into alcohol and VFA, such as acetic, propionic         17 and butyric acids, using acidogenic fermentative bacteria, (ii) a phase of           18 conversion of fatty acids or alcohols into acetic and hydrogen (acetogenesis)        19 using acetogenic bacteria (syntrophic bacteria or OHPA – obligate hydrogen-          20 lis [SOURCE: Page_32]

producing acetogenic – bacteria, homoacetogenic bacteria, sulphate-reducing          21 bacteria), (iii) a final stage of methane production exclusively from acetate,       22 formate, H$_2$ and CO$_2$ using methanogenic bacteria.                                     23 ub [SOURCE: Page_32]

In the case of breweries, it is feasible to use a biogas plant to treat           24 concentrated wastewater with a COD higher than 3.5 g/L. Treating wastewater          25 by anaerobic digestion converts more than 90 % of the initial organic carbon         26 into biogas (CH$_4$, CO$_2$). Fermentation residues (1–5 °% of carbon) require             27 dP [SOURCE: Page_32]

an advanced effluent treatment by aeration. The aerobic step generates 1–3           28 °% of CO$_2$, 1–3 °% of sludge residual and 1 °% organic carbon in the                  29 effluent. An optimised process is used that includes a pre-treatment and a           30 two-step fermentation process. Upstream solid separation and a blending–             31 buffering tank make it possible to separate solid and grainy contraries. This        32 ea [SOURCE: Page_32]

provides a constant wastewater for a steady-going feeding of the fermentation        33 tank. A two-step fermentation process provides the opportunity to exert an           34 influence on single degradation processes that take place in different fermenters.   35 The hydrolysis fermenter (pH = 5.6–6.5) degrades the organic matter by               36 dh [SOURCE: Page_32]

encouraging the action of acidifying bacteria and repressing that of                 37 methanogenic bacteria. Acidified wastewater flows through the methanogenesis         38 fermenter, where biogas is produced. The biogas is a mixture of methane              39 (CH$_4$, 50–85 °% v/v), carbon dioxide (CO$_2$, 15–50 °% v/v) and trace gases              40 o [SOURCE: Page_32]

(H2O, H2S or H$_2$). Before utilisation, water and hydrogen sulphide need to            41 be removed. The calorific value of biogas depends on its CH$_4$ content and             42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_32]

varies between 4 and 7.5 kWh/m^3.                                                     43 In the case of media that are rich in fermentable substrates such as distillery   44 stillage, the acidogenic phase is very active and leads to a high VFA                45 [SOURCE: Page_32]
