960 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_33]

1    concentration (Bories, 1981). Glycerol, a major compound of stillage, is [SOURCE: Page_33]

d 2    easily fermented into propionic acid by propionic bacteria or butyric acid 3    and 1,3-propanediol by clostridia (Claret, 1992; Barbirato et al., 1997; Colin [SOURCE: Page_33]

ite 4    et al., 2001). Since methanogenesis is the limiting stage of the anaerobic 5    digestion of stillage, the equilibrium of the fermentation must be controlled 6    to avoid the accumulation of VFA and the acidification of the digester, which [SOURCE: Page_33]

im 7    would inhibit methanogenesis. The separation of the acidogenic and 8    methanogenic phases in two distinct digesters is a practice proposed to control 9    these phenomena (Ghosh and Klass, 1978; Massey and Pohland, 1978; Bories, 10   1980). The high sulphate contents (the case for molasses stillage and wine [SOURCE: Page_33]

gL 11   stillage treated with calcium sulphate) pose a problem (Karhadkar et al., 12   1987). The sulphate-reducing bacteria form sulphide with a high concentration 13   both in the biogas (3–6 °% in H2S) and in the liquid phase where the free, 14   non-dissociated (H2S) form inhibits anaerobic bacteria at concentrations of 15   about 200 mg S–2/L. The cations (Na+, K+, Ca$_2$+, Mg$_2$+, NH +4 ) are inhibitors 16 17 hin at high concentrations as is sometimes the case in molasses stillage. Often considered as difficult to biodegrade, and even reported to act as inhibitors 18   in biodegradation processes, phenolic compounds, in their monomeric forms, 19   can be degraded by the microflora of anaerobic digestion (Bories and Allaux, 20   1989a,b). For the complex polyphenolic forms, adsorption by the micro- lis 21   organisms in the purification systems leads to a partial elimination. Wine 22   stillage has a high degradation rate (% of eliminated COD) by anaerobic 23   treatment (85–90 %), as does cane juice stillage (90–98 °%), which is not the ub [SOURCE: Page_33]

24   case for molasses stillage that is being studied in many countries in order to 25   optimise fermentation conditions (Decloux and Bories, 2002a). 26 27   Biodigestion technology dP [SOURCE: Page_33]

28   Treatment by anaerobic digestion involves various systems, extensive or 29   intensive, selected in relation to the nature of the wastewater (biodegradability, 30   load) and the industrial context (capacity, seasonality of the production, 31   etc.). 32 ea [SOURCE: Page_33]

33   Treatment in anaerobic lagoons 34   Treatment in anaerobic lagoons is the simplest solution with lagoons at 35   ambient temperature and a long residence time. This method has been applied 36   to cane molasses stillage in India with residence times of 60 to 100 days. dh [SOURCE: Page_33]

37   However, the biogas cannot be recovered. 38 39   Treatment in mixed digesters 40   Treatment in mixed digesters at a controlled temperature was developed in o [SOURCE: Page_33]

41   wine distilleries in Italy in the 1970s and is particularly well adapted to 42   treating stillage with high suspended matter content (lees stillage). The Wo [SOURCE: Page_33]

43   residence times vary from 15 to 25 days and the volume load is from 1–2 kg 44   COD m–3d–1. 45 [SOURCE: Page_33]

The anaerobic contact procedure                                                        1 [SOURCE: Page_34]

d The anaerobic contact procedure involves a mixed digester coupled with the             2 recycling of the microbial biomass separated by static decantation (clarificator).     3 [SOURCE: Page_34]

ite This makes it possible to increase the biomass concentration in the digester           4 and to decrease its volume. The volume load reaches 4–6 kg COD m–3d–1,                 5 and the hydraulic residence time (HRT) varies from six to ten days. Two                6 [SOURCE: Page_34]

im plants, each with two anaerobic contact digesters, have been operating for             7 approximately 15 years in wine distilleries in France (Table 35.11).                   8 9 Fixed biomass on immobile media procedures                                             10 [SOURCE: Page_34]

gL Fixed biomass on immobile media procedures consists of anaerobic digesters             11 where the biomass is immobilised on plastic media with a large developed               12 surface area and a low dead volume. The circulation of the liquid is either in         13 the upflow (anaerobic filter) or downflow direction. These digesters have              14 been developed in wine distilleries (Bories et al., 1982) and molasses distilleries hin                                        15 16 17 Table 35.11 Examples of French anaerobic digestion plants of distillery and winery wastewaters 18 19 20 lis [SOURCE: Page_34]

962 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_35]

1    (Bolivar, 1983; Bories et al., 1988; Bazile and Bories, 1989, 1992). Revico [SOURCE: Page_35]

d 2    (Cognac, France) has two fixed biomass digesters (PVC rings) of 6000 and 3    4000 m^3 for white wine stillage (Table 35.11). However, because of the [SOURCE: Page_35]

ite 4    development of calcium tartrate recovery from pre-concentrated white wine 5    stillage, these anaerobic filters have been converted to mixed digesters to 6    avoid clogging by mineral precipitates of calcium salts. Revico’s anaerobic [SOURCE: Page_35]

im 7    digestion plant comprises four mixed digesters (6000; 5500; 4500 and 3500 8    m^3) at the current time and its capacity is 300 000 m^3 of wine stillage/year 9    (2000 m^3/d) (Table 35.12). The biogas (800 m^3/h) is used on three steam 10   generators for lees and wine distillation and pre-concentration of stillage. [SOURCE: Page_35]

gL 11       The digester of the SIS (Société Industrielle de Sucrerie) distillery in 12   Guadeloupe, with a fixed biomass (PVC rings) and a volume of 1700 m^3 was 13   the first French plant to use anaerobic treatment of cane molasses stillage in 14   1986. A second anaerobic filter (6000 m^3) was added to the plant in 2003. 15   Several dozen similar plants have been set up in molasses distilleries in India 16 17 hin (Proserpol). A distillery in Martinique is presently being equipped with a digester to treat cane juice stillage. The use of lignocellulosic materials as 18   supports for micro-organisms in anaerobic filters has been considered for 19   winery wastewater and cane stillage treatment (Bories and Moulon, 1995; 20   Bories et al., 1997b). lis 21 22   Upflow anaerobic sludge blankets 23   Upflow anaerobic sludge blankets (UASB) are digesters where the liquid ub [SOURCE: Page_35]

24   circulates from the bottom to the top and where the biomass is mobile. Due 25   to a phenomenon of flocculation and agglomeration, the biomass is in the 26   form of granules in the fluid state. These digesters can treat loads of up to 27   30 kg COD m–3 d–1. Digesters in which everything is in circulation make it dP [SOURCE: Page_35]

28   possible to prevent the sludge blanket from clogging (Fama, 2001). 29      Taking the moderate COD concentration (< 5.5 g/L) into account, the 30   easy biodegradability of brewery wastewaters and the high daily volume to 31   be treated, treatment with UASB has been extensively applied, with 265 32 ea [SOURCE: Page_35]

33                Table 35.12 World-wide installations of anaerobic system 34                in the brewing industry. 35 Technology         Number            Area        Number 36 dh [SOURCE: Page_35]

37                Lagoon               3               Africa       18 38                Contact              3               Asia        137 39                Filter               6               Europe      108 Hydrid               5               America     142 40 o [SOURCE: Page_35]

UASB               265 41                EFB                123 42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_35]

Brewing, winemaking and distilling: overview of wastewater           963 [SOURCE: Page_36]

plants, representing 65 % of the total of anaerobic brewery plants in operation      1 [SOURCE: Page_36]

d (Table 35.12) (Totzke, 2005). This process has also been used in Brazil to           2 treat different types of effluent, but very little stillage up until now, although   3 [SOURCE: Page_36]

ite the procedure is technically efficient (Cortez et al., 1999). Because of the         4 limited flow and seasonal production of winery effluent, the development of          5 anaerobic treatment with UASB digesters has been limited (Andreottola et             6 [SOURCE: Page_36]

im al., 1998; Müller, 1998). An example of a treatment plant of mixed winery            7 effluents and stillage with UASB digester is presented in Table 35.11.               8 9 Expanded fluidised beds                                                              10 [SOURCE: Page_36]

gL Expanded fluidised beds (EFB) are based on the microbial colonisation of             11 media (sand, zeolith, etc.) with high specific area (size < mm), which are           12 maintained in suspension by a high recirculating flow. EFB are well suited           13 to the anaerobic treatment of brewery wastewater: a total of 123 digesters           14 (30 % of the total number of anaerobic plants) was reported by Totzke hin                                      15 (2005). UASB and EFB are high-output processes requiring a pre-acidification         16 stage to obtain optimal acidogenesis and to permit the control of the pH in          17 order to avoid inhibition of the methanogenic micro-organisms.                       18 Since 1984, the number of anaerobic facilities for the treatment of brewery       19 effluent has rapidly increased to more than 400 (Table 35.12). Reactor               20 lis [SOURCE: Page_36]

configurations have improved and the spin-off of these projects has led to           21 the widespread application of anaerobic technology in other industries               22 (Vereijken and Driessen, 2001; Totzke, 2005). Industrial anaerobic digestion         23 ub [SOURCE: Page_36]

plants are currently operational and their specificities are fully described in      24 the literature (Ettheridge and Leroff, 1994; Kormelinck, 2003; Nordenskjold          25 and Stippler, 2003; Muroyama et al., 2004; Li and Mulligan, 2005; Pesta and          26 Meyer-Pittroff, 2005; Totzke, 2005).                                                 27 dP [SOURCE: Page_36]

28 Energy optimisation                                                                  29 In addition to the degradation of the organic load, biodigestion produces two        30 utilisable fluids: methane and the effluent still loaded in salts. Methane           31 production by anaerobic digestion results in 350 L CH$_4$/kg degraded COD.              32 ea [SOURCE: Page_36]

The biogas produced has a CH$_4$ content of 60–65 %. This gas (65 % methane)            33 has a net heating value of 1450 kJ/m^3 and can be burned to produce steam or          34 electricity. Depending on the organic load and the nature of the stillage,           35 methane production ranges from 7–20 m^3 CH$_4$/m^3 of stillage. The higher the            36 dh [SOURCE: Page_36]

organic load of the stillage, the closer we get to becoming almost totally           37 energy independent. In wine distilleries, methane production covers almost           38 half of the energy requirements (Bories, 1982). Each year, the Revico plant          39 produces 4000 tonnes equivalent petroleum (TEP) (Menier, 1996). Anaerobic            40 o [SOURCE: Page_36]

digestion of distillery stillage therefore appears to be the primary treatment       41 for effective depollution improving the reduction of BOD from 85 to over 95          42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_36]

%. According to Inamdar (1998) and Shibu et al. (1999), 70 % of distilleries         43 in Asia apparently use biodigestion. According to Nagaraj and Kumar (2006),          44 the post-methanisation effluent from Indian distilleries, if used carefully for      45 [SOURCE: Page_36]

964 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_37]

1    irrigation of agricultural crops, can provide 245 000 t of potassium, 12 500 t [SOURCE: Page_37]

d 2    of nitrogen and 2100 t of phosphorus annually. However, technical, 3    environmental and economic problems still arise when stillage is treated by [SOURCE: Page_37]

ite 4    anaerobic digestion (Cortez et al., 1999) and it requires further treatment in 5    order for the effluent produced, particularly in the case of molasses stillage, 6    to comply with discharge standards. [SOURCE: Page_37]

im 7 8 9    35.3.4 Aerobic treatment 10   In the cases of breweries, aerobic treatment combined with anaerobic sludge [SOURCE: Page_37]

gL 11   stabilisation could be considered for dilute effluents. Biological wastewater 12   treatment in municipal sewage plants is usually an aerobic process, then the 13   sludge surplus can be stabilised by anaerobic digestion. The carbon mass 14   balance indicates that 100 % of organic carbon in the wastewater influent is 15   lost: 50 % through CO$_2$ production in the aerobic step and 50 % through 16 17 hin biomass and sewage production. Sludge is stabilised by anaerobic digestion and generates 28–36 % of biogas (CH$_4$, CO$_2$) and 13–21 % of residual 18   sludge (Pesta and Meyer-Pittroff, 2005). Only 1 % of organic carbon ends up 19   in the effluent. 20       Sludge production and energy costs are the limiting factors in relation to lis 21   the aerobic treatment of concentrated wastewaters, such as those produced 22   by distilleries. The aerobic biological purification parameters of stillage from 23   wine distilleries were studied by Bories and Maugenet (1978), who also ub [SOURCE: Page_37]

24   studied the performance and cost of treatment on an industrial scale. As a 25   result of the heavy organic load, the deficiency in nutrients, the seasonal 26   nature and the variability of winery wastewater production, the design of 27   aerobic wastewater treatment processes has either tended towards extensive dP [SOURCE: Page_37]

28   approaches such as one- or two-stage aerated lagooning (Canler et al., 1998; 29   Racault et al., 1998), aerated storage (Rochard et al., 1998) with different 30   revels of dischange, and mixed treatment with domestic waste (Badie, 1998), 31   and activated sludge (Bolzonella et al., 2006) or towards intensive systems 32   such as two-stage activated sludge (Racault et al., 1998), two-stage bacterial ea [SOURCE: Page_37]

33   filters (Andreottola et al., 2005), or very heavy-load mono-stage pre-treatment 34   (Ehlinger et al., 1994). The study of microbial population dynamics during 35   treatment of synthetic winery wastewater with a rotating biological contactor 36   illustrates the involvement of yeasts and bacteria in the biofilm and the role dh [SOURCE: Page_37]

37   of yeasts in the degradation of the COD (Malandra et al., 2003) that had also 38   been observed by Ehlinger et al. (1994) and Lefebvre (1998). Lalane et al. 39   (1996) and Rols (1996) studied the biological treatment of rum distillery 40   stillage by aerobic digestion, in particular with the system of aeration by o [SOURCE: Page_37]

41   hydro-ejectors. It is possible to reduce 90 % of the soluble COD and more 42   than 95 % of the BOD in only one stage, provided that the pH of the stillage Wo [SOURCE: Page_37]

43   is neutralised, that it is cooled to 30 °C, and that the nutritive balance (nitrogen 44   and phosphorus) is guaranteed, followed by a second stage to reduce the 45   production of sludge. [SOURCE: Page_37]

Brewing, winemaking and distilling: overview of wastewater        965 [SOURCE: Page_38]

The good degradability of the organic load leads to high degradation rates   1 [SOURCE: Page_38]

d for the dissolved COD, and the main problem with aerobic treatment lies in       2 the difficulties related to sludge flocculation and sedimentation. Membrane      3 [SOURCE: Page_38]

ite bioreactors (MBR) are capable of resolving this problem. Artiga et al. (2005)    4 on a pilot MBR with synthetic winery wastewater (diluted white wine, COD         5 < 4 g O$_2$/L) obtained a high output (97 % COD) and a low residual COD (<          6 [SOURCE: Page_38]

im 100 mg O$_2$/L); however, the accumulation of biomass in the reactor decreased      7 the oxygenation capacity. The combination of aerated storage and membrane        8 filtration offers new treatment possibilities for small wineries (#3000 hL)      9 (Racault and Stricker, 2004). For distillery wastewater with a low organic       10 [SOURCE: Page_38]

gL load (1 g COD/L), Zang et al. (2006), studied a calefactive (30–45 °C) aerobic   11 MBR equipped with a stainless steel membrane (0.2 µm). The COD removal           12 efficiency was 94.7 % with a HRT of 10–30 h and a volumetric load rate of        13 0.6–2.8 kg COD m–3 h–1.                                                          14 hin                                  15 16 35.3.5 Pre and post-treatments                                                   17 Although biological treatments are well suited to the degradation of dissolved   18 organic load, the presence of suspended matter and complex substances such       19 as phenolic compounds, melanoidins, etc., particularly in stillage, has led to   20 lis [SOURCE: Page_38]

the design of pre- or post- physicochemical treatments. Molasses stillage        21 from the digester still has a COD of 30–40 g/L equivalent to that of products    22 usually treated in digesters. Numerous studies deal with post-treatment, an      23 ub [SOURCE: Page_38]

obvious necessity.                                                               24 25 Coupling anaerobic digestion with an aerobic treatment                           26 Most of the authors referred to have studied the combination of anaerobic        27 dP [SOURCE: Page_38]

digestion followed by an aerobic treatment, which makes it possible to reduce    28 the BOD to about 0.5 g/L and the COD to about 5 g/L for molasses stillage        29 (Inamdar, 1998) or malt whisky wastewater (Uzal et al., 2003). The final         30 effluent can then be discharged into the river (Maiorella et al., 1983). The     31 use of a membrane reactor for this final stage of aerobic degradation could      32 ea [SOURCE: Page_38]

be worth exploring. However, in certain cases, the colour of the effluent is     33 still too dark (Shibu et al., 1999).                                             34 35 Degrading the colouring and recalcitrant COD by micro-organisms                  36 dh [SOURCE: Page_38]

Various laboratory studies have been conducted on the biodegradation of the      37 recalcitrant compounds in stillage. They have shown that certain micro-          38 organisms (the fungi Deuteromycetes, Basiodimycetes, Eurotiomycetes) enable      39 the partial elimination, under specific conditions, of these compounds from      40 o [SOURCE: Page_38]

molasses stillage undergoing anaerobic and aerobic digestion, with Coriolus      41 (Trametes) versicolor, Aspergillus sp (Ohmomo et al., 1985, 1987;                42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_38]

Sirianuntapiboon et al., 1988a,b; Gonzales Benito et al., 1997; Shayegan et      43 al., 2005). García García et al. (1997) suggested carrying out the aerobic       44 treatment with Aspergillus terreus or Geotrichum candidum before the             45 [SOURCE: Page_38]

966 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_39]

1    anaerobic treatment, in order to reduce the phenol concentration from 60 to [SOURCE: Page_39]

d 2    70 %. Research on the selection of strains capable of destroying these pigments 3    continues (Fitz-Gibbon et al., 1998; Nakajima-Kambe et al., 1999; Patil et [SOURCE: Page_39]

ite 4    al., 2001) as well as studies aimed at understanding the degradation mechanism 5    (Miyata et al., 1998). The aerobic degradation of beet molasses stillage with 6    Penicillium sp strains and Aspergillus niger, before anaerobic digestion, [SOURCE: Page_39]

im 7    resulted in a degree of higher COD removal and increased the decolourisation 8    of the wastewater (Jiménez et al., 2003). Finally, Shibu et al. (1999) showed 9    that the bacteria Lactobacillus casei reduces the colouring by 54–57 % and 10   results in a simultaneous production of lactic acid in batch fermentation over [SOURCE: Page_39]

gL 11   five days at a rate of 113 mg/L of lactic acid with immobilised cells. Lactic 12   acid has a market in India, since 70 % is imported from other countries such 13   as Japan. 14       This research shows the microbiological perspectives of biodegrading 15   recalcitrant forms of COD in stillage, but implementing these cultures on an 16 17 hin industrial scale still seems a long way off for treating molasses stillage. Contrary to the majority of studies on the degradation of colours with aerobic 18   cultures, Mohana et al. (2007) isolated a bacterial consortium from soil that 19   contains Pseudomonas aeruginosa PAO$_1$, Stenotrophomonas maltophila and 20   Proteus mirabilis, and that is able to decolourise anaerobically-treated spent lis 21   distillery wash under static conditions. The colouring matter and the recalcitrant 22   COD are less of a problem in the case of wine stillage than in molasses 23   stillage. However, the polyphenolic compounds from grape (anthocyanins, ub [SOURCE: Page_39]

24   tanins) contribute to the final colouring and the residual COD of the treated 25   effluent. The bioremediation of winery waste by means of white-rot fungi 26   has recently been reported (Strong et al., 2006). 27 dP [SOURCE: Page_39]

28   Decolouration by ozonation and/or oxidative treatment 29   Dhamankar et al. (1993) studied ozonation and showed that it is more effective 30   when sodium hydroxide is added to modify the pH (decolourising of 26 %, 31   68 % and 92 % at a pH of 4.3, 7 and 10, respectively) in the presence of 32   1.2 % H2O$_2$. Gehringer et al. (1997) studied different modes of ozonation (alone or combined with γ rays). Beltrán et al. (1999) also showed that ea [SOURCE: Page_39]

33 34   ozonation of wine stillage improves its biodegradability and makes its 35   subsequent decolouration more complete. However, degradation levels are 36   highly dependent on the pH of the wastewater because pH affects the double dh [SOURCE: Page_39]

37   action of ozone on the organic matter, that may be a direct or an indirect (free 38   radical) oxidation pathway (Beltrán et al., 2001). The degradation of phenolic 39   compounds is not necessarily complete, but it contributes to the bleaching of 40   the effluents and improves the biodegradability of the degradation products o [SOURCE: Page_39]

41   (Bijan and Mosheni, 2005). The inclusion of an ozonation step prior to 42   treatment in an anaerobic sequencing batch reactor was found to be useful Wo [SOURCE: Page_39]

43   for the treatment of cherry stillage, since more than 75 % of the polyphenols 44   could be removed by ozone and an improvement in the parameters of the 45   anaerobic treatment (COD removal rate, higher organic load rate (OLR), [SOURCE: Page_39]

ite rate of the anaerobic digestion by 25 % (Martín et al., 2002).                     4 5 Decolouration by treatment on activated coal or nanofiltration                     6 [SOURCE: Page_40]

im Serikawa et al. (1993) showed that it is possible to remove the colour from        7 dilute stillage (from 1 to 0.1 % weight) on activated coal, but the procedure      8 is long and nothing was mentioned about the cost of regenerating the coal.         9 Cartier et al. (1997) showed that the colorants in the brine used to regenerate    10 [SOURCE: Page_40]

gL the decolourising resins of syrup are effectively retained by nanofiltration       11 (NF), whereas the saline fraction passes into the permeate. We can therefore       12 hope that the colouring of stillage before or after anaerobic digestion will       13 also be retained, especially since Jaouen et al. (2000) succeeded with pen         14 inks.                                       hin                                    15 16 Physicochemical treatments                                                         17 The clarification of lees stillage can be achieved, for example, with              18 centrifugation upstream of the heat concentration step or by flocculation/         19 flotation upstream of anaerobic digestion. Sales et al. (1986) studied the         20 lis [SOURCE: Page_40]

precipitation of acids with sodium hydroxide or lime coupled with separation       21 by centrifugation. The treatment is valid on lees stillage where the deposit       22 contains more than 80 % of the COD. Similarly, Pandiyan et al. (1999)              23 ub [SOURCE: Page_40]

studied the addition of ferrous sulphate (FeSO$_4$·7H2O) and ferric chloride          24 (FeCl$_3$·6H2O) in stillage in order to precipitate propionic acid. According to      25 Lalov et al. (2000), anaerobic digestion is apparently not well suited to          26 solutions that are not particularly concentrated, such as wine stillage, for       27 dP [SOURCE: Page_40]

example. They therefore studied the concentration of organic matter by retention   28 on biodegradable anionic exchangers made of chitosan and its biodigestion          29 with or without prior hydrolysis, after saturation with organic acids.             30 Photocatalytic oxidation with Fenton’s reagent (mixture of H2O$_2$ and Fe$_2$+)          31 has recently been studied for winery wastewater pre-treatment and total            32 ea [SOURCE: Page_40]

organic carbon (TOC) removal reached 50 % (Mosteo et al., 2006a,b).                33 Experiments on the laboratory scale were carried out to reduce colour and          34 COD in distillery wastewater using electro-oxidation processes (anode made         35 from a titanium sponge, pH = 1, additives: H2O$_2$ and NaCl) with stillage            36 dh [SOURCE: Page_40]

diluted 10-fold (Piya-areetham et al., 2006). 968 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_41]

1    recommended level for discharge into a river. Coupling the anaerobic digestion [SOURCE: Page_41]

d 2    of stillage with a secondary treatment by thermal evaporation of digested 3    stillage provides an interesting solution to this problem. The SIS distillery in [SOURCE: Page_41]

ite 4    Guadeloupe initiated treatment by thermal evaporation of cane molasses 5    stillage produced by anaerobic digestion in 2004. The condensate resulting 6    from the evaporation presents a very high level of purification in terms of [SOURCE: Page_41]

im 7    COD, colour, mineral content and suspended matter. The concentrate from 8    digested stillage can be highly concentrated because of its low organic load, 9    and is used for agronomic purposes (spreading, composting). The thermal 10   concentration of effluent from anaerobic digestion of wine stillage has been [SOURCE: Page_41]

gL 11   recently achieved at the UCVA distillery (Coutras, France) for secondary 12   treatment in order to obtain high-quality final wastewater for discharge into 13   a river. The condensate from the evaporator (15 t/h, multiple effects) that 14   treats digested stillage (pomace, wine, lees) has a low COD (< 300 mg/L) 15   and is colourless, demineralised and has no suspended matter or micro- 16 17 hin organisms. The energy for the thermal evaporation is provided by steam generators using biogas produced at the anaerobic stage. 18 19 20   35.3.6 Incineration lis 21   In the brewing process, spent grain is a by-product (0.18–0.20 kg/L beer) 22   with a high water content (70–80 % w/w). The constraints involved in using 23   it as ruminant cattle feed or landfill material were described on pp. 000–000 ub [SOURCE: Page_41]

24   Brau Union Autria and Loeben university (Kepplinger and Zanker, 2001) 25   developed a process associating the combination of mechanical pre-drying 26   and combustion in a biomass vessel. The wet spent grains (20–30 % w/w 27   DM) are stored in a butter vessel to compensate for fluctuating production dP [SOURCE: Page_41]

28   and then press-filtered up to 42 % w/w DM. The dried matter is stable and 29   can be stored in a tank before combustion in a biomass vessel. The wastewater 30   could be processed by anaerobic treatment. In the process proposed by Heineken 31   Technical Service and 2B Biorefineries (Bruijn et al., 2001; Schwencke, 32   2006), the fibres extracted from spent grain could be sent to a furnace, where ea [SOURCE: Page_41]

33   the heat of combustion from the fibre product is used to generate steam. In 34   both processes, the heat of combustion of the spent grains is similar to that 35   of lignite coal or dry wood, i.e. approximately 21 MJ/kg. The exact heat of 36   combustion depends on the water content because of the relatively high dh [SOURCE: Page_41]

37   vaporisation energy of water: H = 21 000 · (1 – w) – 2250 · w. Above 40 % 38   w/w DM, the combustion properties improve considerably. Combustion also 39   produces ash, which is another valuable product. Its high phosphorus pentoxide 40   (P2O$_5$) content is of great value as a fertiliser additive and can be added to o [SOURCE: Page_41]

41   standard NPK-fertilisers. Wet cleaning of the flue gas is normally not necessary, 42   the discharge of exhaust gases (NOx, SO$_2$, CO$_2$) that are emitted from the Wo [SOURCE: Page_41]

43   combustion of spent grains or fibres is below the standards set by the European 44   governments. 45       In alcohol production, the incineration of stillage can be an attractive [SOURCE: Page_41]

Brewing, winemaking and distilling: overview of wastewater          969 [SOURCE: Page_42]

means of recovering mineral matter and energy with the total combustion of         1 [SOURCE: Page_42]

d organic matter, and it seems to be common practice in India (Inamdar, 1998;        2 Nagaraj and Kumar, 2006). In this process, the raw spent distillery wash is        3 [SOURCE: Page_42]

ite first neutralised with lime and filtered. This is further concentrated to 60 %     4 solids in multiple-effect with forced circulation evaporators. Then this thick     5 liquor is burnt in an incinerator and converted to ash. The heat of combustion     6 [SOURCE: Page_42]

im of the liquors is 8600 kJ/kg solids (Maiorella et al., 1983; Nagaraj and Kumar,    7 2006) and a positive return in energy can be obtained. The resulting ash is        8 found to contain about 37 % potash (K2O) and 2–3 % phosphate (P2O$_5$), and           9 their reuse makes it possible to balance the economic viability of the process.    10 [SOURCE: Page_42]

gL However, special boilers are necessary, firstly to recover the ash and secondly    11 to limit the temperature to below that of potassium sulphate fusion which is       12 only about 700 °C. Because of increasingly strict air pollution guidelines,        13 incineration has to be considered carefully for any new proposal, which            14 should include an electrostatic precipitator system. In Australia, direct hin                                    15 combustion of cane stillage was carried out for several months but was             16 finally abandoned (Nguyen, 2003).                                                  17 18 19 20 lis [SOURCE: Page_42]

35.4 Alternative treatments and re-engineering                                     21 processes with the best available techniques                                       22 (BAT) approach: industrial reality and                                             23 ub [SOURCE: Page_42]

24 alternative treatments 25 Implementing environmental management systems in the brewing, winemaking           26 and distilling industries requires the efficient and effective integration of      27 dP [SOURCE: Page_42]

risks and opportunities. The Integrated Pollution Prevention and Control           28 directive 96/61/EC (EC, 1996) is a key stage in environmental legislation          29 and defines BAT selection. The word ‘available’ in this context means available    30 under circumstances which are both economically and technically viable,            31 and ‘techniques’ means not only the technology but also its operation on the       32 ea [SOURCE: Page_42]

ground. Any BAT candidate judged to be positive in terms of environmental          33 benefits must then be studied with respect to its effects on product quality,      34 food, land occupation and industrial safety. Its economic impact needs to be       35 assessed and this depends on existing or new plants and their size.                36 dh [SOURCE: Page_42]

The environmental impact is analysed on different geographical scales           37 (global, regional, local) and can be divided into three groups: availability of    38 (resources water, fossil fuels, raw materials, chemicals), nuisance factors        39 (emission of noise, odour, and dust) and toxic effects (health considerations).    40 o [SOURCE: Page_42]

Alternative treatments and re-engineering processes and techniques (Table          41 35.13) are proposed for the brewing, winemaking and distilling industries.         42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_42]

However, a wide heterogeneity in development levels is noticeable from             43 laboratory scale up to industrial application. ‘Real issues’ or differences        44 between industrial reality and scientific/academic approaches must be identified   45 [SOURCE: Page_42]

Woodhead Publishing Limited; proof copy not for publication [SOURCE: Page_42]

Brewing, winemaking and distilling: overview of wastewater          971 [SOURCE: Page_44]

and taken into consideration when assessing any of these alternative 1 [SOURCE: Page_44]

d technologies.                                                                       2 3 [SOURCE: Page_44]

ite 4 35.4.1 Industrial reality                                                           5 Treatment of effluents containing alcohol and sugars                                6 [SOURCE: Page_44]

im The presence of ethanol as the major component of effluent generated by the 7 alcoholic beverage industry (wine, etc.), and of sugars in the case of canneries 8 for fruit and sweetened beverages (fruit juice and syrup) underscored the 9 specificity of their composition and made it possible to find treatments adapted 10 [SOURCE: Page_44]

gL to their specific makeup (Bories et al., 1998; Bories, 2000). In the case of 11 winery effluents, distillation of the effluent alone is an effective treatment 12 (elimination of COD: # 85 %), making it possible to discharge the dealcoholised 13 effluent into the wastewater system and to recover the ethanol (Colin et al., 14 2005). The combination of distillation and concentration of the dealcoholised 15 hin effluent ensures a highly effective and complete treatment with production 16 of: (i) purified evaporation condensate (COD < 300 mg/L, demineralised, 17 bleached, germ-free) that can be reused as industrial water or discharged 18 directly into the receiving environment; (ii) ethanol (energy recovery); and 19 (iii) a concentrated co-product (> 5 % of the initial volume of effluent) that 20 lis [SOURCE: Page_44]

can be spread, composted or used in distilleries (recovery of tartaric acid) 21 (Bories et al., 1998, 1999; Colin et al., 2005).                                    22 For effluents containing sugars (glucose, fructose, sucrose, maltose), the 23 ub [SOURCE: Page_44]

transformation of sugars into alcohol must be done beforehand. Alcoholic 24 fermentation can be initiated at the level of effluent storage by yeast inoculation 25 (S. cerevisiae). Thanks to the use of mechanical steam compression, energy 26 consumption for concentration and distillation is reduced (15–20 kWh/m^3). 27 dP [SOURCE: Page_44]

For effluents with a heavy organic load (COD > 20 g/L), it is competitive 28 with that of biological treatment processes. This process, operational at maximal 29 load as soon as it is started up and insensitive to variations in the pollution 30 load, is the solution to the problem of seasonal activities. The absence of 31 wastewater sludge is another important advantage of this physical fractionation 32 ea [SOURCE: Page_44]

technique applied to effluents.                                                     33 34 Extraction of specific molecules or compounds                                       35 Separation of glycerol, betaine and organic acids                                   36 dh [SOURCE: Page_44]

Stillage contains large quantities of glycerol, betaine in the case of beet 37 stillage and organic acids. The glycerol is commonly used in industry as a 38 solvent, emollient and antifreeze. The betaine is used in the pharmaceutical 39 industry as a complement to other compounds against muscular deficiencies 40 o [SOURCE: Page_44]

and weakness, as a complement in animal feed (enables water retention in 41 the muscle tissues) and in crop protection. Glycerol can be separated by 42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_44]

precipitation with lime (CaO) or by ethanol treatment. Cheryan and Parekh 43 (1995) have studied the separation of glycerol from the organic acids of 44 molasses stillage by electrodialysis after a prefiltration on a 0.2 µm ceramic 45 [SOURCE: Page_44]

Woodhead Publishing Limited; proof copy not for publication [SOURCE: Page_44]

972 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_45]

1    membrane. However, it is chromatography techniques which have been [SOURCE: Page_45]

d 2    developed on the industrial scale, particularly with regard to molasses. 3    Numerous authors have published on the subject with patents pending (Kampen, [SOURCE: Page_45]

ite 4    1990; Kampen and Saska, 1999a, b) for the University of Louisiana. Most of 5    the patents involve stillage concentration phases, potassium removal by 6    crystallisation, clarification and one or several chromatography techniques [SOURCE: Page_45]

im 7    depending on the number of compounds to separate. 8 9    Extraction of tartaric acid 10   Tartaric acid is present in all wine distillery effluents (Mourgues et al., 1996) [SOURCE: Page_45]

gL 11   and represents from 4–30 % of the pollutant load. Extracting tartaric acid 12   from lees stillage by precipitation in the form of calcium tartrate salt is a 13   widespread practice in wine distilleries (Mourgues and Maugenet, 1975; 14   Mourgues et al., 1993). Moreover, the recovery of tartaric acid is essential 15   before concentrating stillage. To precipitate tartaric calcium salt, the stillage 16 17 hin is first made neutral with calcium carbonate milk or quick lime to pH 4.5– 5, then calcium sulphate (CaSO$_4$) is added to have a full precipitation and to 18   avoid the potassium tartrate (K2C4H4O$_6$) formed during the neutralisation 19   process from remaining in solution. There are two main types of procedure, 20   which have been described by Mourgues (1986). Distilleries recover 4–6 kg lis 21   of tartaric calcium salt per hL of lees received. The products obtained contain 22   48–53 % of tartaric acid. Particular attention must be paid to the impact of 23   tartaric acid extraction on subsequent treatments, in particular biodigestion ub [SOURCE: Page_45]

24   where the sulphate can indirectly inhibit fermentation. It is therefore preferable 25   to reduce the tartaric acid extraction rate but to avoid adding sulphate ions if 26   biodigestion takes place. However, in order to maintain optimal recovery of 27   tartaric calcium salt and to prevent the formation of soluble tartaric potassium dP [SOURCE: Page_45]

28   salt, the sulphate (a mixture of lime and calcium sulphate) is replaced by 29   nitrate (lime and nitric acid). Moreover, this process change is advantageous 30   for the treatment of lees stillage by natural evaporation, since nitrate reduces 31   the production of odorous compounds (Bories, 2006). Other procedures for 32   extracting tartaric acid have been studied, as explained below. ea [SOURCE: Page_45]

33      Liquid–liquid extraction has been envisaged in the laboratory using wine 34   effluent and synthetic solutions of tartaric, malic or lactic acids with the 35   solvents tributyl-phosphate-n-dodecane and triisocytlamine-octanol-1 36   (Smagghe, 1991; Malmary et al., 1994; Marinova et al., 2004). It has the dh [SOURCE: Page_45]

37   advantage of eliminating the intermediate precipitation in the form of tartaric 38   calcium salt. 39 40   Extraction of colouring anthocyanic matter and o [SOURCE: Page_45]

41   phenolic compounds 42   Mourgues et al. (1996) mentioned the separation of colouring matter from Wo [SOURCE: Page_45]

43   grape pomace before distillation, either by extraction by diffusion in the 44   presence of SO$_2$ or by adsorbing resins. The industrial production of 45   concentrated anthocyanic extracts (E163) has rapidly expanded these past [SOURCE: Page_45]

Brewing, winemaking and distilling: overview of wastewater       973 [SOURCE: Page_46]

years in wine distilleries (Salgues, 1980; Usseglio-Tomasset, 1980). The 1 [SOURCE: Page_46]

d production of antioxidant extracts with nutraceutical properties constitutes a 2 new way of using wine by-products (Shrikhande 2000; Tobar et al. 2004). 3 [SOURCE: Page_46]

ite 4 Loss reduction with ultra and microfiltration                                   5 In breweries, loss reduction concerns mainly beer recovery from tank bottoms 6 [SOURCE: Page_46]

im (fermentation and maturation vessels). The membrane-separated permeate 7 can be recycled in the wort or in the maturation vessels (Reed, 1989; Nielsen, 8 1989) for fermentation tank bottoms. The beer recovered from the maturation 9 tank bottom may be returned into the maturation vessel or sent for final 10 [SOURCE: Page_46]

gL clarification. However, the different compositions of the tank bottom beer 11 may prevent a direct dilution into the rough beer before filtration (Cantrell 12 et al., 1985; Le, 1987; O’Reilly et al., 1987). Tank bottom concentrates may 13 be sold as livestock feed.                                                      14 Two fundamental differences exist among tank bottoms: (i) the fermentation 15 hin vessels have high yeast cell content and high viscosity; (ii) the maturation 16 vessels have high protein and polyphenol content, and fewer yeast cells and 17 are characterised by low viscosity (close to that of beer). In order to recover 18 ‘green beer’ and ‘rough beer’ from tank bottoms, natural sedimentation, 19 centrifugation and a filter-press may be used. However, centrifugation is 20 lis [SOURCE: Page_46]

expensive and may damage the permeate quality because of yeast cell 21 degradation. Filter-presses provide a relatively low-moisture solid discharge 22 and consequently high extract recovery. However, sufficient clarification of 23 ub [SOURCE: Page_46]

the filtrate is not obtained. The use of micro filtration (MF) is designed to 24 produce: a permeate of acceptable quality with respect to both flavour and 25 haze (defined by the European Brewery Convention norm, Analytica EBC, 26 1987), with minimal loss of original gravity, colour and bitterness while 27 dP [SOURCE: Page_46]

processing a retentate of between 2 and 4 % dry weight to a minimum of 20 28 %; to operate at low temperatures (close to 0° C); to achieve economically 29 sound flux and hygienic beer recovery. The presence of cloudiness or haze 30 in beer is one of the more obvious quality defects discernible to the consumer. 31 Several substances can cause haze in beer, but the most frequently encountered 32 ea [SOURCE: Page_46]

problem is due to a cross-linking of polyphenol (tannin) and protein.           33 Almost all the membranes installed in breweries around the world are 34 dedicated to the recovery of beer from fermentation and maturation tank 35 bottoms. These membrane applications have almost become industrial 36 dh [SOURCE: Page_46]

standards. The biggest challenge today is more a problem of commercialisation 37 than a food-engineering problem. Since 1994 numerous industrial applications 38 (Methner et al., 2004; Fillaudeau et al., 2006) have been reported in addition 39 to scientific papers. Micro filtration enables a 20–30 % w/w concentration to 40 o [SOURCE: Page_46]

be reached, and several industrial units already use it. More than 50–60 % of 41 the yeast sediment is recovered as a high-quality beer (equivalent to a volume 42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_46]

reduction ratio of between 2 and 3). Membrane filtration becomes competitive 43 in comparison to the filter-press for waste reduction. The recovered permeate, 44 recycled in the brewing process at a rate of 2–5 %, allows beer loss and costs 45 [SOURCE: Page_46]

Woodhead Publishing Limited; proof copy not for publication [SOURCE: Page_46]

974 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_47]

1    to be reduced. Various systems are in use and it has been shown that ceramic [SOURCE: Page_47]

d 2    (0.4–0.8 µm, Schlenker, 1998) or polysulfone (0.6 µm, Wenten et al., 1994) 3    membranes concentrate solids from 12–15 % to 20–22 %. The payback is [SOURCE: Page_47]

ite 4    less than two years regarding the recovery of sterile beer from yeast beer 5    with 0.4–0.8 µm pore diameter multichannel ceramic membranes installed 6    in 1 MHL capacity breweries. Bock and Oechsle (1999) explained that brewing plants are running with ceramic membranes made of α-aluminium oxide [SOURCE: Page_47]

im 7 8    (multichannel membrane: 19 channels, length: 1020 mm, mean pore diameter: 9    0.80 µm). Surplus yeast can be processed with about 17–20 L h–1 m–2, up to 10   a concentration of 20 % w/w (transmembrane pressure up to 3 bar) and three [SOURCE: Page_47]

gL 11   process options exist: batch, semi-batch and continuous. This material can 12   be cleaned in place since it is resistant to caustic, acid and oxidising sterilants 13   even at high temperature (above 90° C). 14       Snyder and Haughney (1999) and Methner et al. (2004) described a new 15   system called VMF (vibrating membrane filtration) produced by PallSep™ 16 17 hin (Pall Corporation, USA). The system differs from traditional cross-flow filtration systems in that the shear at the membrane surface is generated 18   mechanically by vibrational energy and not from high cross-flow rates. VMF 19   enables uncoupling of pressure differential from cross-flow velocity, with a 20   reduced installed pump capacity, a minimum energy input, reduced mechanical lis 21   and thermal stressing of yeast cells and a compact design of filter module 22   operating without backwashing. The system operates with a transmembrane 23   pressure (TMP) of 500–800 mbar, with 0.45 µm polytetrafluoroethylene (PTFE) ub [SOURCE: Page_47]

24   membranes, under an oscillation of around 50 Hz and amplitude of 20 mm 25   at the outer rim (diameter 800 mm). Recovery of beer from surplus yeast can 26   achieve an average flux of 18–22 L h–1 m–2 with an industrial module of 27   40 m^2 (energy input: 6 Wh/L) with solids concentration of 10.5–18 % w/w. dP [SOURCE: Page_47]

28 29   Process modification with ultra and microfiltration 30   In breweries, MF can be utilised as a technological alternative in three 31   applications: mash separation, clarification of rough beer, cold-sterilisation 32   of clarified beer before conditioning. Scientific studies and industrial ea [SOURCE: Page_47]

33   applications essentially concern the clarification of rough beer and sterile 34   filtration of clarified beer. Modrok et al. (2006) reported that the filtration 35   technologies in breweries use diatomaceous earth (91 %), trap filters (68 %), 36   sheet and fine filters to reduce the level of micro-organisms (32 %) and dh [SOURCE: Page_47]

37   sterile filtration with membranes (8 %). 38 39   Cold-sterilisation of clarified beer 40   The clarification of rough beer is usually followed by heat treatment so as to o [SOURCE: Page_47]

41   ensure its microbiological stability and conservation. Currently, heat treatment 42   is mainly performed by flash pasteurisation (72–74 °C during 15–30 s with Wo [SOURCE: Page_47]

43   a plate heat exchanger or at 60 °C in a tunnel pasteuriser) before conditioning. 44   Conventional heat treatment requires water loops to heat and cool the product 45   and also induces additional water and energy consumption. [SOURCE: Page_47]

Brewing, winemaking and distilling: overview of wastewater            975 [SOURCE: Page_48]

Sterile filtration appears interesting and eliminates the organoleptic problems   1 [SOURCE: Page_48]

d caused by heat processing (Gaub, 1993; Leeder, 1993). Micro filtration will           2 have to face several challenges: to produce a microbe-free beer without a             3 [SOURCE: Page_48]

ite negative change in beer quality, whilst operating at low temperatures (close          4 to 0 °C); to ensure beer stability (biological, colloidal, colour, aroma and          5 flavour, foam stability); to achieve economic flux. Provided it fulfils these         6 [SOURCE: Page_48]

im considerations, MF can be a truly operational alternative to pasteurisation           7 and dead-end filtration with cartridges. Cold-sterile filtered beer (draught          8 beer or bottled beer) corresponds to a strong demand from consumers for               9 quality and natural products. The objective of eliminating heat treatment of          10 [SOURCE: Page_48]

gL the finished product is achieved with membrane cartridge systems (dead-end            11 filtration) installed directly upstream of the filling system. However, cold-         12 sterilisation by cross-flow membrane is under trial and is feasible in an             13 industrial context (Fillaudeau and Carrère, 2002; Scanlon, 2004). Krottenthaler       14 et al. (2003) reported that the technical developments of membrane filtration hin                                       15 (membrane lifetime, running time, cleaning procedure, cost reduction) as              16 well as market indicate constant improvement. Organic membrane filtration             17 (0.45 µm nylon or 0.55 µm polyvinylidene fluoride, PVDF) offers safe and              18 careful product stabilisation for the brewing industry. Financially MF is             19 becoming increasingly attractive; for instance the cost of flash pasteurisation       20 lis [SOURCE: Page_48]

is assumed to be 0.20 7/hL whereas membrane filtration is around 0.26 7/              21 hL of clarified beer.                                                                 22 23 ub [SOURCE: Page_48]

Clarification of rough beer                                                           24 Beer clarification is probably one of the most important operations, when             25 rough beer is filtered in order to eliminate yeast and colloidal particles            26 responsible for haze. In addition, this operation should also ensure the biological   27 dP [SOURCE: Page_48]

stability of the beer. It should comply with the haze specification of a lager        28 beer in order to produce a clear bright beer. Standard filtration consists of the     29 retention of solid particles (yeast cells, macrocolloids, suspended matter)           30 during dead-end filtration with filter-aids. The variety of compounds (chemical       31 diversity, large size range) to be retained makes this operation one of the           32 ea [SOURCE: Page_48]

most difficult to control. However, membrane processes should satisfy the             33 same economic and qualitative criteria (O’Reilly et al., 1987; Wackerbauer            34 and Evers, 1993) as conventional dead-end filtration. Micro filtration should         35 be able: to produce a clear and bright beer with similar quality to a Kieselguhr      36 dh [SOURCE: Page_48]

filtered beer; to perform separation in a single-step without additives; to           37 operate at low temperature (0 °C); to achieve economic flux.                          38 Among the potential applications of cross-flow microfiltration, the               39 clarification of rough beer represents a large potential market (approximately        40 o [SOURCE: Page_48]

200 000 m^2 surface area of membrane). Industrial experiments, however,                41 encountered two main problems: (i) the control of fouling mechanisms and              42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_48]

(ii) the enhancement of permeate quality (Fig. 35.3). Micro filtration suffers        43 from a low permeate flux in comparison to the conventional dead-end filtration        44 with filter-aids such as diatomaceous earth (usual flux ranges from 100–              45 [SOURCE: Page_48]

976 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_49]

1    500 L h–1 m–2). Since 1995, a lot of reports have mentioned the economic [SOURCE: Page_49]

d 2    and scientific stakes of the clarification of rough beer. Recent scientific and 3    industrial studies (e.g. Fillaudeau et al., 2007) have dealt with (i) fouling [SOURCE: Page_49]

ite 4    mechanisms, (ii) the relationship between quantitative and qualitative 5    performance, (iii) the development of alternative membrane filtration such 6    as membrane structure and dynamic filtration and (iv) industrial applications. [SOURCE: Page_49]

im 7        Since 2000, the first industrial plants have started to run with three membrane 8    systems proposed by Norit Membrane Technology/Heineken Technical Service 9    (Schuurman et al., 2005a,b), Alfa-Laval AB/Sartorius AG (Modrok et al., 10   2004, 2006), and Pall Food & Beverage/Westfalia Food Tech (Denniger and [SOURCE: Page_49]

gL 11   Gaub, 2004; Höflinger and Graf, 2006; Rasmussen et al., 2006). Norit/ 12   Heineken (Schuurman et al., 2005a,b) reported several industrial processes 13   running with a MF unit for rough beer clarification with a capacity above 10 14   000 L/h. The filtration unit contains between 10 and 24 hollow fibre modules 15   X-Flow R-100 (pore size: max 0.50 µm, length: 1 m, inner diameter: 1.5 mm, 16 17 hin filter area: 9.3 m^2, material: polyethersulfone, PES). The key to the process is based on a specific cleaning procedure patented by Heineken and Norit 18   Membrane Technology. It combines a caustic step, an acidic step and a 19   strong oxidative step (two hours in duration), which is successful in achieving 20   a run time between seven and 20 hours for about 120 runs. Filtration is lis 21   accomplished at 0 °C, 1.5–2 m/s flow velocity and up to 1.6 bar transmembrane 22   pressure. During filtration, 10 minute periods of back-flushing are applied 23   every two hours to remove the reversible fouling that has built up. The flux is maintained at 100 L h–1 m–2 and clarified beer fulfils the European Brewery ub [SOURCE: Page_49]

24 25   Convention (EBC) standard in terms of turbidity (close to 0.6 EBC units), 26   bitterness, total extract, colour, and protein content. In 2005, the cost of 27   membrane filtration for bright beer was estimated to be between 0.20 and dP [SOURCE: Page_49]

28   0.40 7/hL, i.e. identical to Kieselguhr filtration 0.20–0.40 7/hL. By 2007, 29   the total cost of membrane filtration is expected to be 20–30 % cheaper than 30   Kieselguhr filtration (Schuurman et al., 2003). 31       In the Alfa-Laval/Sartorius cross-flow filtration process (Modrok et al., 32   2004, 2006), the rough beer goes from the maturation tank to a high- ea [SOURCE: Page_49]

33   performance centrifuge, which is directly followed by the cross-flow system. 34   From there the beer goes to a bright beer tank and then on to sterile cartridge 35   filtration before conditioning. The filtration unit contains up to six holding 36   devices with up to 72 filter Sartocon® cassetes (20 membranes, dimension: dh [SOURCE: Page_49]

37   175 × 210 mm, small channel spacers: 120 µm, filter area: 0.7 m^2, material: 38   PES). Filtration steps are accomplished with a combination of normal filtration, 39   feed reverse to loosen the clogging and back-flushing with the product. An 40   intermediate cleaning (duration: 15 min) is done every three to five hours o [SOURCE: Page_49]

41   and maintains high and constant flux rates (80–120 L m–2 h–1). The costs are 42   estimated at 0.46 7/hL and can roughly be divided into 22 % for the running Wo [SOURCE: Page_49]

43   costs, 48 % for the membranes, and 30 % for the system. 44       PROFi® technology is a joint project of Westfalia Food Tec and Pall Food 45   & Beverage (Denniger and Gaub, 2004; Höflinger and Graf, 2006; Rasmussen [SOURCE: Page_49]

Brewing, winemaking and distilling: overview of wastewater            977 [SOURCE: Page_50]

et al., 2006) and is based on a combination of a centrifuge and a hollow fibre        1 [SOURCE: Page_50]

d membrane filter system. The centrifuge separates most of the coarse solids            2 like yeast and colloids with a high dry substance from the beer; the membrane         3 [SOURCE: Page_50]

ite system afterwards separates the remaining yeast and fine-forming colloids             4 effectively. The membrane system is a patented polyethersulfone hollow                5 fibre cross-flow system operating in a dead-end mode. No retentate tank or            6 [SOURCE: Page_50]

im recirculation line is necessary, which makes the system design and control            7 simple. The industrial system is designed to reach a constant flux of 36–48           8 000 L/h and consists of five independent and identical blocks operating in a          9 sequential mode (three to four blocks in filtration mode, one or two blocks           10 [SOURCE: Page_50]

gL in cleaning and standby mode). Operating runs last between five and ten               11 hours, if one block has reached the maximum pressure difference of 2 bar, it          12 is emptied and cleaned. Beer losses for the complete line are at 0.02 %               13 extract; the water consumption is surprisingly low with 0.043 L/L beer and            14 energy consumption less than 0.40 kWh/hL.   hin                                       15 16 17 35.4.2 Alternative treatments including scientific                                    18 and academic approaches                                                               19 Regenerable filter-aids                                                               20 lis [SOURCE: Page_50]

In breweries, reduction of Kieselguhr consumption may be achieved by                  21 optimising the existing process in different ways (Freeman and Reed, 1999):           22 selection and characteristic of filter-aids, pre-coating and multistage-filtration,   23 ub [SOURCE: Page_50]

automation of filtration system and filter-aid dosage, increasing filtration          24 capacities, saving water for cleaning and regeneration by chemical and thermal        25 treatment. However, the use of regenerated Kieselguhr appears to be of                26 limited occurrence in industrial practice. The opportunity to carry out the           27 dP [SOURCE: Page_50]

filtration with alternative and regenerable filter-aids seems very attractive.        28 The filter-aid should satisfy food process requirements, resist caustic solutions     29 and temperatures up to 100 °C (conventional regenerative conditions), exhibit         30 specific mechanical properties (inert and rigid material), present a low specific     31 surface area but a high retention capacity (clarification) together with a high       32 ea [SOURCE: Page_50]

filtration efficiency. Regeneration of the spent filter medium should not             33 modify its initial performances. Recent results have been reported at a pilot-        34 plant scale but none in industrial conditions. Below, we describe the filter-         35 aids used by Bonachelli et al. (1999) and Rahier and Hermia (2001).                   36 dh [SOURCE: Page_50]

The regenerable filter-aid developed by Interbrew and UCL (Université             37 Catholique de Louvain, Belgium) is composed of polymer granules (Rahier               38 and Hermia, 2001) with specific properties (density, particle size, pore size,        39 diameter, shape and specific surface). The material, in combination with              40 o [SOURCE: Page_50]

poly vinyl poly pyrrolid one (PVPP), was used successfully for the clarification      41 and stabilisation of beer. The advantages reported for this material are a            42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_50]

single clarification–stabilisation step with high specific flow rate and long         43 run times.                                                                            44 Meura company (Bonachelli et al., 1999) developed a filter-aid composed           45 [SOURCE: Page_50]

978 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_51]

1    of a mixture of synthetic polymer or special cellulose fibres and 44–88 µm [SOURCE: Page_51]

d 2    microbeads coated with a polymer which improves surface properties. The 3    mixture combines the mechanical properties of the microbeads [SOURCE: Page_51]

ite 4    (incompressibility, low porosity) with the qualities of the fibres. Filtration 5    performance is reported to be similar to conventional Kieselguhr filters. 6 [SOURCE: Page_51]

im 7    Bioproduction of added-value molecules 8    Industrial and agricultural by-products and waste can often be used as substrates 9    in fermentation processes. Their complex composition, containing carbon, 10   nitrogen and mineral supplies, is accurate for the growth of micro-organisms. [SOURCE: Page_51]

gL 11   The aim of the bioprocess may be the production of biomass, or its metabolic 12   products (i.e. organic acids), flavour and aroma compounds or enzymes. The 13   carbon components of stillage can be considered as substrates for the production 14   of molecules of interest to industry via biotechnological pathways. Tibelius 15   and Trenholm (1996) have published a whole report on recycling the co- 16 17 hin products from cereal fermentation and Decloux and Bories (2001) a literature survey on uses for stillage from molasses fermentation. They mentioned 18   several examples of bioproduction of added value molecules that may be 19   grouped in three categories 20 lis 21   Yeast, enzymes and algae 22   The production of yeast in aerated medium is an efficient means for reducing 23   the pollutant load of stillage originating from alcohol production either from ub [SOURCE: Page_51]

24   cane or beet. This technique, developed on an industrial scale, however, 25   consumes a lot of energy to ensure the oxygen supply and the cooling of the 26   fermenter. It is possible to produce 16 kg of Torula yeast (Candida utilis) per 27   tonne of stillage and to consume non-fermentable sugars, hence increasing dP [SOURCE: Page_51]

28   the ratio which can be recycled to fermentation and, similarly, to decrease 29   the quantity of water to be evaporated during concentration. The residual 30   BOD is reduced to 10–15 g/kg (Maiorella et al., 1983). According to Lee and 31   Lee (1996), Candida utilis yeasts, generally used for producing SCP (single 32   cell proteins) are not very well adapted to stillage. After screening tests, they ea [SOURCE: Page_51]

33   selected a thermoresistant strain Candida rugosa. Shojaosadati et al. (1999) 34   studied the culture of the Hansenula yeast strain in continuous culture on 35   beet stillage and showed that it is possible to reduce the COD by 31 % and 36   to produce 3–5 g/L of biomass with a protein content of 39.6 % without any dh [SOURCE: Page_51]

37   addition to the culture medium. Other compounds such as glycerol, acetic 38   acid and the rest of the ethanol can also be consumed (Maiorella et al., 39   1983). 40      The production of enzymes is also under study. In breweries, Zvauya and o [SOURCE: Page_51]

41   Zvidzai (1996) found that an aerobic and spore forming Bacillus sp. produces 42   hydrolytic extracellular enzymes when cultured on opaque brewery wastewater Wo [SOURCE: Page_51]

43   supplemented with defatted soya, spent yeast and malt flour. The strain 44   produced endo-1-4-α-glucanase, amylase, polygalacturonase, xylanase and 45   protease. Hatvani and Mecs (2001) investigated the mycelial growth (biomass [SOURCE: Page_51]

Tests for producing            10 [SOURCE: Page_52]

gL fungi on stillage have apparently been carried out at the laboratory stage in           11 Brazil (Cortez et al., 1998).                                                           12 The culture of filamentous fungi has been studied in wine distillery stillage        13 (white wines) in the mid 1970 s and an industrial unit was created following            14 this research (Biovina/Remy Martin, Cognac), but it only operated for a hin                                         15 short period. The culture of green algae in Turkey has been tested to produce           16 pigments from a medium enriched in molasses stillage (Kadioglu and Algur,               17 1992).                                                                                  18 19 Organic acids                                                                           20 lis [SOURCE: Page_52]

Commercial utilisation of natural ferulic acid has been limited by its availability     21 and cost. It can be used as a preservative due to its ability to inhibit peroxidation   22 of fatty acids, and constitutes the active ingredient in many skin lotions and          23 ub [SOURCE: Page_52]

sunscreens. Faulds et al. (1997) isolated and purified a number of novel                24 microbial esterases, which can cleave ferulic acid from sugar residues in               25 agro-industrial waste. They showed that after treatment of wheat bran with              26 a Trichoderma fungus, followed by treatment of the dissolved material with              27 dP [SOURCE: Page_52]

Aspergillus niger FAE-III, ferulic acid can be obtained. L-lactic acid production       28 from brewery spent grain with immobilised lactic acid bacteria, Lactobacilus            29 rhamnosus, was investigated by Shindo and Tachibana (2004). Spent grains                30 were liquefied by a steam explosion treatment (30 kg/cm^2, 1 min) to obtain              31 liquefied sugar (60 g/kg wet spent grain) and treated with glucoamylase,                32 ea [SOURCE: Page_52]

cellulase and hemicellulase enzymes before bioreaction.                                 33 In propionibacteria (Propionibacterium acidipropionici), the fermentative            34 pathway of glycerol leads to the production of propionic acid in very                   35 advantageous conditions with regard to the results obtained from glucidic               36 dh [SOURCE: Page_52]

substrates: increase in yield and propionic acid concentration (Barbirato et            37 al., 1997; Bories et al., 1997a 2001; Himmi et al., 2000). ite 4    production by fermentation of glycerol by anaerobic bacteria (Clostridium 5    butyricum and Enterobacter agglomerans) has been examined using wine 6    distillery stillage (Bories and Claret, 1992; Barbirato et al., 1998). [SOURCE: Page_53]

im 7       The Revico company has applied for a patent to produce aromatic 8    compounds (Ambid et al., 1998; de Billerbeck et al., 1999). It involves 9    aerobic cultivation of a Sporobolomyces odorus type bacterium capable of 10   producing γ-decalactone. The medium is constituted of wine stillage [SOURCE: Page_53]

gL 11   supplemented with a ricinoleic-type precursor. The aromatic compound is 12   separated from the aqueous fermentation medium by adding a coconut oil 13   type lipid phase which is solid at room temperature and which absorbs the 14   aroma. After separation, the lipid phase is dissolved in 96 % ethanol (1v/ 15   10v) then separated out by crystallisation on cooling the alcohol mixture to 16 17   containing the aromatic compound. hin –20 °C. Simple filtration then makes it possible to recover the alcohol phase [SOURCE: Page_53]

18      Carotenoids, in particular astaxanthin, can be produced by fermentation 19   of the yeast Phaffia rhodozyma on different residues of the wheat industry 20   (Hayman et al., 1995). Certain co-products such as soluble stillage can be lis 21   interesting media. Cell growth and polysaccharide production by a local 22   strain of Ganoderma lucidum was studied using thin stillage with an added 23   carbon source (Yang et al., 2003; Hsieh et al., 2005). ub [SOURCE: Page_53]

24      Biosurfactants are beginning to be accepted as potential performance- 25   effective molecules that are ecofriendly alternatives to synthetic surfactants. 26   Economic strategies, which emphasise the utilisation of waste streams as no- 27   cost substrates are essential for developing large-scale biosurfactant production dP [SOURCE: Page_53]

28   technology. It has been reported that biosurfactant production from distillery 29   and whey wastewaters and synthetic medium was comparable using 30   Pseudomonas aeruginosa strain BS$_2$ (Dubey et al., 2005). 31      Bioplastic production by micro-organisms was investigated by Yu et al. 32   (1998) with malt waste from a brewery. Specific polymer production yield ea [SOURCE: Page_53]

33   by Alcaligenes latus DSM1124 increased up to 70.1 % w polymer/w cell 34   with a final biomass and polymer concentration of 32.36 g/L cell dry wt and 35   22.68 g/L cell dry wt. In this fermentation, biopolymer accumulation is 36   controlled by nitrogen limitation. dh [SOURCE: Page_53]

37 38   Extraction of specific compounds 39   Recovery of dissolved molecules and water 40   As the cost of wastewater disposal increases, more emphasis is being placed o [SOURCE: Page_53]

41   upon the recovery and recycling of valuable chemicals contained within the 42   effluent. As mentioned by Decloux and Bories (2001), a lot of research has Wo [SOURCE: Page_53]

43   been carried out into the recovery of molecules using MF to NF and reverse 44   osmosis membranes (Wu et al., 1989; Kim et al., 1997; Nataraj et al., 2006). 45   Kim et al. (1997) proposed a new process for producing alcohol from wheat, [SOURCE: Page_53]

Brewing, winemaking and distilling: overview of wastewater           981 [SOURCE: Page_54]

associating a centrifuge separation and a stillage ultrafiltration (UP) stage.   1 [SOURCE: Page_54]

d Permeate is recycled for the preparation of the fermentation must. The retentate 2 is recycled to the head of the centrifuge separator. The only output is the      3 [SOURCE: Page_54]

ite cake, which comes out of the centrifuge separator and which, after drying,       4 can be used in animal feed.                                                      5 Numerous articles were published between 1985 and 1990 by Wu (research       6 [SOURCE: Page_54]

im centre in Illinois, USA) on the recovery of dissolved and nitrogenous matter     7 from pre-filtered and centrifuged stillage originating from the fermentation     8 of different raw materials including beet (Wu et al., 1989). Treatment on an     9 UF membrane then RO makes it possible to concentrate the dissolved matter        10 [SOURCE: Page_54]

gL and the nitrogenous matter in a small volume (final volumetric reduction         11 ratio, VRR between eight and four) and obtain water with a lower conductivity    12 than tap water. Nataraj et al. (2006) tested a hybrid NF and RO pilot plant to   13 remove the colour and the contaminants of spent molasses distillery              14 wash. Colour removal by NF and rejection of 99.8 % TDS, 99.9 % of COD hin                                  15 and 99.99 % of potassium was achieved from the RO runs, by retaining a           16 significant flux as compared to the pure water flux, which shows that            17 membranes were not affected by fouling during the wastewater run. The            18 pollutant levels in permeates were below the maximum contaminant levels          19 as per the guidelines of the World Health Organization and the central pollution 20 lis [SOURCE: Page_54]

board specifications for effluent discharge (less than 1000 ppm of TDS and       21 500 ppm of COD). The paper does not indicate the composition of the final        22 retentate (mixture of NF retentate and RO retentate) or the applicability on     23 ub [SOURCE: Page_54]

a large scale.                                                                   24 Other studies were carried out on the recovery of water from condensates     25 generated during stillage concentration. The condensates were used to dilute     26 the molasses. It was quickly observed that the kinetics of the fermentation      27 dP [SOURCE: Page_54]

was decreased and even completely stopped. Analysis of the condensates           28 demonstrated the concentration of molecules that inhibit fermentation was        29 high. Morin et al. (2003) demonstrated that the molecules responsible were       30 31 mainly aliphatic acids (formic, acetic, propionic, butyric, valeric and hexanoic), alcohols (2,3 butanediol), aromatic compounds (phenyl-2-ethyl-alcohol) and       32 ea [SOURCE: Page_54]

furane derivatives (furfural). These are small molecules present at low          33 concentrations in the system. Anaerobic digestion experiments and RO             34 experiments were carried out to choose an appropriate treatment for the          35 condensates. Preliminary results showed that most of the organic compounds       36 dh [SOURCE: Page_54]

were degraded by anaerobic treatment, but not completely, and a subsequent       37 filtration by RO was necessary. Direct RO experiments with the condensates       38 showed good but not total rejection of the molecules (Morin-Couallier et al.,    39 2007). Increasing the pH of the condensates nearly achieved total retention.     40 o [SOURCE: Page_54]

Research continues on both treatments.                                           41 42 Wo [SOURCE: Page_54]

Extraction of salts by electrodialysis                                           43 Electrodialysis tests in the laboratory showed that it is possible to reduce the 44 potassium concentration of beet stillage by 92 % (Decloux et al., 2002). 45 [SOURCE: Page_54]

Woodhead Publishing Limited; proof copy not for publication [SOURCE: Page_54]

982 Handbook of water and energy management in food processing [SOURCE: Page_55]

1    Then concentration up to 70 % solids should be possible without potassium [SOURCE: Page_55]

d 2    sulphate crystallisation. Nevertheless, considering the sale cost of concentrated 3    vinasses as fertiliser, the investment costs still do not allow industrial [SOURCE: Page_55]

ite 4    application. 5 6    Elimination of heavy metals [SOURCE: Page_55]

im 7    Plant-derived materials may be used to adsorb heavy metals, but many reviews 8    report the efficiency of micro-organisms (fungi, algae, bacteria). The ability 9    of micro-organisms to remove metals from solutions is well known, and 10   both living and dead biomass is capable of metal accumulation. Effluents [SOURCE: Page_55]

gL 11   from many industries contain metals in excess of permitted levels. Biomass 12   use may be economically feasible. Wang and Chen (2006) report that 13   biosorption may constitute a cost-effective biotechnology for the treatment 14   of high-volume and low-concentration complex wastewaters containing heavy 15   metal(s) in the order of 1–100 mg/L. Among the promising biosorbents for 16 17 hin heavy metal removal which have been researched during the past decades, Saccharomyces cerevisiae has received increasing attention due to the unique 18   nature in spite of its mediocre capacity for metal uptake compared with other 19   fungi. S. cerevisiae is widely used in food and beverage production, is easily 20   cultivated using cheap media, is also a by-product in large quantity as a lis 21   waste of the fermentation industry, and is easily manipulated at molecular 22   level. Dostalek et al. (2004) report the sorption of cadmium, Cd$_2$+, copper, 23   Cu$_2$+ and silver ions, Ag+. Marques et al. (1999) found that waste brewery ub [SOURCE: Page_55]

24   biomass of non-flocculent and flocculent types are promising biosorbents 25   for the removal of Cu$_2$+, Cd$_2$+ and Pb$_2$+ at concentrations of up to 1.0 mM 26   from non-buffered aqueous solutions. Runping et al. (2006) studied the 27   influence of the uptake of Cu$_2$+ and Pb$_2$+ by waste beer yeast in different dP [SOURCE: Page_55]

28   adsorptive conditions (pH, contact time, yeast concentration, temperature, 29   ion concentrations) to compare the biosorption behaviour of a single-metal 30   system and a two-system in batch mode. The process of biosorption nearly 31   reached equilibrium in 30 min and the optimum pH was near 5.0. Beer yeast 32   absorbed 0.0228 mmol g–1 for Cu$_2$+ and 0.276 mmol g–1 for Pb$_2$+. ea [SOURCE: Page_55]

33      Extraction studies on laboratory and pilot scales from wine and by-products 34   (pomace) have focussed on novel molecules such as RG-II 35   (rhamnogalacturonan II), which have metal complexing properties (Vidal et 36   al., 1999). dh [SOURCE: Page_55]
